# OriCode

A native macOS window for coding agents, running Claude Code today. Swift and SwiftUI, one pane of glass, nothing on it but the conversation.

This file is the whole plan. There is no tracker, no roadmap doc and no design doc beside it. When something changes, it changes here.

## What this is

Claude Code's work with Codex's quiet. You open the app, the desktop shows through the window, you type, Claude answers, you approve what it wants to do, you read the diff. That's the product.

It is a personal client and a Swift learning project with a real spec. It is not trying to beat the official Claude Code desktop app on features; that app already has every panel a person could want. This one has almost none, on purpose.

## What this is not

Not an ADE, not an orchestrator, not a board of cards that run themselves. Not a web app, not cross-platform, not a phone app. Not a terminal app, a browser or an IDE, though its composer turns into a shell prompt behind ⌘J or a `!`. When one of those seems necessary, open the official app for that task and come back.

## Rules that don't move

1. **Never hand-roll a control the platform already has.** `Picker`, `Toggle`, `Slider`, `Menu`, `List`, `TextField`, the `Settings` scene, `NSOpenPanel`, `UserNotifications`, native materials, the native window. The previous version of this app was a web view, and over a thousand lines went into redrawing dropdowns, toggles and window chrome that AppKit gives away. It still looked wrong. If a card starts to read as "build a custom X", the card is wrong; fix the card.
2. **No persistent panels.** The only things always on screen are the transcript and the composer. Threads, changes, files, settings: each slides over the same glass when called and leaves when done. Keyboard summons it, a click elsewhere or Esc dismisses it.
3. **The app never touches an Anthropic credential.** No API-key field, no token, no login screen. The engine runs the Claude Agent SDK, which uses the `claude` the user already logged into in Terminal. If that login is missing, the app says "run `claude` in Terminal and log in" and nothing more. The previous version settled on this as the line that keeps a personal client inside Anthropic's terms; keep it.
4. **One window, dark glass.** No light mode. The desktop's colour coming through the blur is the app's colour. Nothing in the interface has a border; surfaces come apart by tint and by space.
5. **Every card leaves a working build.** A card that ends with the app not launching is not done.
6. **Fast, light and small.** OriCode is meant to be the quickest, smoothest agent window there is. With nothing working it idles at no CPU: nothing polls, animates or wakes the Mac unless the user is looking at it or waiting on it. A frame is never dropped under the pointer, a keystroke never waits on the disk or the engine, and nothing is added to the app or the engine that it doesn't need. A card that touches a path the user feels, launch, typing, scrolling a transcript, a turn streaming, measures it before and after, and says so in its Notes.

## How to work this board

Take the first card under **Todo**. Move it under **In progress** by editing this file. Build it. Check the *Done when* line by building and running the app (`make run`), not by reading the code. Move the card under **Done** with the commit hash. Commit as `K-NN: <card title>`, one card per commit. Do not start the next card while the build is red. Since 2026-09-24 the repo is public at github.com/realmeric/OriCode: once a card's commits are in, including the one that records its hash, push main.

If a card turns out to be two, split it here before starting. If you learn something that changes a later card, edit that card now and say so in the commit message. If a *Done when* needs a human eye (K-10 does), build it, stop, and tell Meriç exactly what to look at.

Every time rule 1 is broken on purpose, add a line under **Exceptions** at the bottom with the reason. Two exceptions is a smell. Three means stop and re-read the rules.

Versions stay under 0.1.0 until the first public release on GitHub, which is 0.1.0. Until then a release is 0.0.N, where N is its release card's number, and its tag is `v0.0.N`. Release cards up to K-61 say v0.1 to v0.7, the numbers they had when they were done; their tags were renamed in K-64.

0.1.0 went out on 2026-09-24 (K-122), and the freeze 0.1.0-rc began (K-121) ended with it. New work comes from Linear's OriCode project, the 0.2.0 milestone first, and becomes a card here when it's taken.

## Design brief

The reference is the empty Codex window: a rounded pane of glass with the wallpaper's orange and violet visible through it, traffic lights top-left, a single mark in the middle, and no chrome at all. The test is whether the window still feels like that once a conversation is in it.

**Window.** A unified toolbar with its background hidden, full-size content, `isMovableByWindowBackground` so any empty glass drags the window. Traffic lights stay where macOS puts them in a toolbar window, with one thing beside them: the sidebar button, the toolbar's only item. At the other end of that row sits the review's button, plus-slash-minus and the working tree's counts in green and red, laid out on the lights' line the way the capsule is, since a toolbar with its title hidden has no trailing side of its own. The material is Liquid Glass, an NSGlassEffectView, in its dark variant since the app is always dark: with nothing set it looks like the rest of the Mac and follows System Settings › Appearance › Liquid Glass. Settings' Glass lays black over it, from none at System to half at Dark, and Transparency fades the glass down to a quarter so the desktop shows through sharp. Corner radius is the system's. Minimum size 720×480, default 1180×760.

**Surfaces.** Composer: white at 12% with a 1pt inset highlight of white at 12% along its top edge, which is what makes it read as raised glass rather than a different material. User message: white at 7%, radius 18, right-aligned, max width 560. Card (diff, ask, a thread's latest limit, Claude's plan): white at 5%, radius 14. Drawer: white at 6%, radius 14. Hover on a row or an icon button: white at 7%. Selected row: white at 10%. An action button is a capsule of white at 10%, 16% under the pointer or pressed, and the one Return presses is white at 80% going to full white with dark words; disabled, a button shows at 40%. A composer with something held over it to drop: white at 16%. That is the entire palette of surfaces. Settings is a window of the same glass, laid out like Meriç's reference: a sidebar of panes under a search field, the selected one in the system's grey accent, holding the traffic lights the way the drawer does, and each pane's settings in cards under quiet headings, rows parted by hairlines inside a card, with native pop-ups and switches and the app's action buttons. Those hairlines are the one place a line appears, because the reference has them.

**Ink.** System font. Body 14pt, secondary 12.5pt, monospace SF Mono 12.5pt. White at 92% for text, 55% for secondary, 30% for faint. Colour carries meaning and nothing else does: a soft green for added lines and a soft red for deleted ones; kullanym-notch's amber on plan usage from half a window used and its red from 70%, because they say a limit is near, on the glass and on the number alone in a thread's near-limit line, and that red filling a limit's card's glass to its rim, because there the limit is reached; and in the model picker on the cost of Max and Ultracode, in the band the session is in now, since there they say it before you choose to spend faster, while usage with room left stays white; each project's badge colour, picked at random from eight when the project is added, because it says whose thread a row is; and Claude's orange on Claude's own mark beside the model's name and on the effort rail and the level's name, because effort is how hard Claude thinks: the fill is the orange at full strength at every level, since thinned it mixes with the glass and reads brown, and it pales toward ember over a stretch before the thumb that grows with the level; the stops it has passed light as ember lamps; the thumb is a bead lit from below by its level's heat, white only at the top of the scale; and what moves in the rail glows warm, sparks born white-hot and cooling to the orange. Fast mode's bubbles in the mark's head are the same heat: Claude's orange on its white levels, white-hot on its orange ones. No accent colour: the system's accent is a neutral grey, dark enough to carry white words, for what macOS draws with it: a selected row in a sidebar, a default button, a switch that's on, a menu's highlight and selected text. A working thread's mark, in the drawer and at the title capsule's end, lights its dot while the main loop runs and a ray for each agent at work, in white; each head keeps its ray, and commands never light one.

**Layout.** Transcript is a centred column, max 760pt wide, with 20pt side padding at narrower widths. The composer is a capsule at the bottom of that column, 28pt from the window's bottom edge, minimum height 48pt, radius 24pt, and it grows with the text up to 40% of the window. In a thread with nothing in it, the composer sits in the middle of the window under the mark, and the first message sends it down to its place. Left to right it holds the text, an attach button, the model button (Claude's mark, the model's short name, a bolt in fast mode, and the level it runs at, faint when it's Default's; it opens a card of the composer's own glass that rises out of the composer's right end, or drops below it when there's no room above: Fast at the top left, the model's name, which turns to a page of models with the ones starred first under Favorites and the account's older ones under More models, scrolling past the effort page's height, and Back to Defaults; OriCode's mark, whose dot grows and heats with the level, holds bubbles in fast mode, and whose rays light at Ultracode; the level's name and its line; the effort slider, whose ringed stop is Default and whose last stop, past a gate, is Ultracode; and the permission modes as tiles), the usage glass, an 18pt disc of the send button's tint that fills from the bottom as the session goes, inside a 2pt rim of that tint, and send: a 36pt circle that becomes Stop while a turn runs and nothing is typed. With something typed it stays the arrow, and Return or a click sends it into the running turn, where it waits under the transcript as your bubble in secondary ink, with "Waiting for the next step" faint under it, until Claude takes it up and it brightens in place. ⌥Return, or the key Settings › Shortcuts gives the queue, puts it in the thread's queue instead, for after the turn, as the arrow's help says: queued messages sit inside the composer's glass above the field and any thumbnails, a line each in the order they'll go, the first line of each in secondary ink with a cross that's faint until hovered; three and a half lines at most, the rest scrolling under a fade; a click takes one back into the field. Stop hands both back, what was sent into the turn and what was queued, oldest first and ahead of what's typed. Between two pieces of Claude's text, the tool calls it made, with any thinking among them, fold into one row that says what they did in Claude Code's words, "Read 2 files, ran 3 commands", with the lines its edits added and removed, and opens to the calls themselves; a lone call keeps its own line, and an ask ends a run. Claude's plan gets a card where it was first written, which ends a run too: a glass that fills as items get done beside "3 of 7 done", then its items in order, a done one ticked and faint, the one in progress lit and saying what it's doing, a pending one plain; each later TodoWrite updates that card in place, the item that changed fading to its new state, and has no line of its own, so the run around it stays one row. A list written after the last was all done, or with fewer than half its items in the last one, starts a new card. A workflow Claude starts gets a card of its own where its call is, which ends a run too: its name beside the mark, whose rays light for its agents at work and turn while it runs, how far it has got, and its phases, each with a bead per agent, faint while queued, lit while running, settled once done and red if it failed; a click lists the agents under their phases. Under a turn, a footer says how many files it changed and by how much; the time it took and what it cost are there only if Settings asks for them. A `!` at the start of the composer, or ⌘J, turns it into a shell prompt for the thread's folder, a `$` before the text in SF Mono, until Esc, ⌫ in an empty prompt or ⌘J again: the `$` grows out of the composer's left end as the text moves over for it, the text and placeholder cross-fade into SF Mono, and it goes back the same way. There Tab completes the word being typed as the user's own shell would, zsh's own completion when that's their shell and otherwise a command or a path from the thread's folder, with several matches listed above the composer for Tab to go through, and ↑ brings back earlier commands; in a message Tab completes a slash command or a path after `@`, as the agent reads it, and ↑ in an empty composer takes back the last queued message first and, with none queued, brings back earlier messages. Tab never takes the keyboard out of the composer. Return runs the command in a terminal of its own, and its block lands in the transcript on a card: the command, then what it printed in the terminal's colours, following the output while it runs, with Stop. Claude reads what each block has printed since it last read it with your next message; a slash command goes out alone and leaves them for the message after. The block Continue in Claude Code runs the thread's session in is never read. A block still running once the transcript has scrolled past it is named in the gap under the composer, with Show and Stop. A program that takes the whole screen, vim or less, opens its block into a full terminal over the conversation's side of the window, up to 900pt wide and down to 12pt above the composer, in the Nerd Font a prompt's icons need when one is installed and SF Mono otherwise, and puts the block back in the thread when it quits; Open on a running block does the same, to type into it. Keys go to the program, Esc too, and Close, ⌘J or a click on the transcript put the block back. An open block belongs to its thread: another thread keeps its own composer and asks, and coming back finds the block open. While a thread works, its mark sits at the title capsule's left end. ⌘K's command center, ⌘I's heads, ⌘P's file finder and ⌘⇧D's review stretch down out of the title capsule, the way the Dynamic Island grows into what it's showing, and fold back up into it as they go: one at a time, each with its top where the capsule's is, in the capsule's lane between the traffic lights and the review's button, 20pt from a pinned drawer. A click on the mark grows the heads too, and the mark moves into their header: the main loop with what it's on, then each agent with its step, time, tokens and tool calls, each workflow with its beads, and each command with a `$` and its last line; Stop is on the row under the pointer, and that row's rays brighten in the header's mark. Typed into, ⌘K finds what was said too: under its ranked rows a Messages section lists the messages and replies that hold every word typed, a snippet of each beside its thread's badge and name, and Return opens the thread at that message, lit white at 10% for a moment. The review is up to 960pt wide and reaches down to 12pt above the composer, or in a thread with nothing in it yet, whose composer waits in the middle, 20pt above the window's bottom; an open block and the review put each other away, and a file opened from the review puts it away. ⌘K, ⌘P and the review draw over an open file. It tells the working tree's diff by turn: your message in the transcript's bubble, then the turn's files, each open one a card with its header on top and its hunks inside, parted by space, each hunk with both line numbers, the words that changed lit in a stronger green or red, and a reviewed hunk folded to one faint line. A file folds to its header with a click, and the first time the review opens in a thread only the first file with something to review is open; the keyboard reads one file at a time, opening each as it gets there and folding the one it leaves.

**The drawer.** The thread list is a drawer, not a sidebar. Closed, the window is only the conversation. Bring the mouse to the left edge, or onto the sidebar button right of the traffic lights, and it slides in over the glass; move away and it slides back. While it's out, the composer's left end draws back out from under it and its right end stays put. Press ⌘1–9 and it slides in just long enough to show the thread you picked: that row lit and nudged 6pt to the right, the way one card stands proud of a drawer of index cards, then it slides back on its own. A click on a row nudges it the same way, for the same 700ms. Clicking the sidebar button, or ⌘B, pins it open for people who want a list, and doing it again puts it away wherever the pointer is.

Numbers: 280pt wide, running the window's full height 6pt in from its top, left and bottom edges, corners 12pt so they sit concentric with the window's, and the traffic lights and the sidebar button sit in the middle of its first 40pt, the way ChatGPT's sidebar holds them. The window has a toolbar that draws nothing for this: AppKit centres the lights in its 52pt row, the sidebar button is its only item, and the title capsule sits on the same line. Hot zone 20pt at the window's left edge, 120ms before it reacts. Slide in 220ms ease-out, slide out 150ms ease-in, and it waits 150ms after the mouse leaves before going. The ⌘digit peek lasts 700ms after the keypress, or as long as the mouse is over it. Rows 34pt: the project's badge, the title (single line, truncated), the rays mark while the thread works or waits, a pin on a pinned thread, and the ⌘digit for the first nine. The list holds every project's threads: the pinned ones first, in the order they were put, with 10pt of space under them, then the rest, a new one on top and the others where they were dragged. A thread joins the list with its first message. Rows drag to reorder; dropped among the pinned ones a thread is pinned there, and a pinned one dropped below the rest's first row isn't any more. Under the title-bar row, a `Menu` for the project new threads start in, with its badge, and "Add project…" at the end, and at the header's right an Add project button; at its foot, New thread on the left and a Settings gear on the right, both lit on hover like a row.

**Motion.** `.spring(duration: 0.28, bounce: 0.12)` for anything that moves, `.easeOut(duration: 0.18)` for anything that appears or fades. Nothing bounces more than that, with three exceptions borrowed from kullanym-notch for things that travel or measure: the composer's slide from the middle to the bottom, and back up to the middle once an emptied thread's transcript has faded, and the usage card use its glide (`.spring(response: 0.5, dampingFraction: 0.86)`), and the usage glass fills or empties to a new reading with its reading spring (`response: 0.9, dampingFraction: 0.9`), because a gauge that snaps reads as a glitch. Nothing animates that the user didn't cause, except what reports work the user started: rays lighting; and, while the model picker is open, the motes, slugs, embers, sparks, streaks and turning rays of a level or speed the user chose, drawn by Core Animation for a few seconds after a change or while the pointer moves along the rail, the rays coasting to rest upright; in fast mode, bubbles swirling inside the mark's head for those same seconds and then resting where they are, and at Ultracode the rays going round in half a second with a trail of earlier copies behind each, coasting to rest over 1.2 seconds; and at every level a change arrives, light running up the fill into the thumb and flaring the lamps it passes as the level rises, and the heat draining back into the thumb as it falls, in under a second below Extra high. The effort thumb's settle carries the drag's speed into the move spring. With Reduce Motion on, what grows out of the capsule, and the prompt out of the composer, fades in and out in place instead. The trackpad taps only under a moving finger, never for a click or a key: a stop crossed on the effort rail, harder arriving at Max or Ultracode, something held over the composer that can be dropped there, and a slider caught at its default.

**Empty state.** The mark, 44pt, centred, and under it one line: "Where do we pick up?" for a project, "Add a project to start." with none. The mark is Meriç's: a dot inside six arcs. The dot is the thread's main loop and the arcs are rays, each lit ray an agent at work in the thread, a workflow's agents included, up to six; idle, they rest at 30% white around a 92% dot. The app icon is the same mark on a dark violet squircle.

**Words.** Where the app speaks for itself it names no provider: OriCode is a native window for coding agents, a thread works, asks and is sent notes, and in Auto the model decides what is safe. Claude and Claude Code are named where they are the subject: signing in, the CLI's version and settings, the plan's usage, the models beside Claude's mark, their errors and retries in a thread, and Continue in Claude Code.

## Architecture

**App.** Swift 6, SwiftUI, macOS 26 deployment target (Liquid Glass materials). If the Mac turns out to be on macOS 15, drop the target to 15 and use `.ultraThinMaterial`; K-01 records the real numbers. The Xcode project is generated from `project.yml` with XcodeGen so the whole thing builds from the command line; `OriCode.xcodeproj` is gitignored and regenerated. A `Makefile` fronts everything: `make run`, `make engine`, `make test`, `make app`. Debug builds are OriCode Molten, com.realmeric.oricode.molten, whose store, actions, review marks and logs live in folders named after the build (the Info.plist's OriCodeFolder), so a change can be tried beside the OriCode it's being made in.

**Engine.** A TypeScript sidecar in `engine/`, built on `@anthropic-ai/claude-agent-sdk`. Node 24 runs TypeScript directly, so there is no build step: the app spawns `node engine/main.ts` and talks newline-delimited JSON over stdin/stdout. It ships inside the app bundle under `Contents/Resources/engine/` with its `node_modules` (`npm ci --omit=dev` at build time). The app finds `node` by asking a login shell (`zsh -lc 'command -v node'`), then `/opt/homebrew/bin`, `/usr/local/bin`, `~/.nvm/versions/node/*/bin`, and insists on 24 or newer; Settings can override the path. The app starts it at utility QoS, which its CLIs inherit, and points ORICODE_CACHE at `Engine/` in its Application Support folder. Read the SDK reference before writing a line of it; the shapes below are the app's protocol, not the SDK's.

**Updates.** Sparkle (K-135) with a user driver of OriCode's own, `Updates`, whose phase the update circle beside the title capsule draws. The feed is `appcast.xml` on main, read from raw.githubusercontent.com, and names only the newest release, whose zip is a GitHub release asset signed with the EdDSA key in Meriç's login keychain; `SUPublicEDKey` in the Info.plist is its public half. Builds are numbered by the commits on main (`CURRENT_PROJECT_VERSION`), so a newer release always has a higher `CFBundleVersion`. `make release` (scripts/release.sh) does the publishing. Before signing, it and `make app` run scripts/prune.sh, which takes out Sparkle's XPC services, nibs and translations, unused by a sandbox-free app with its own driver, and every Highlightr theme but two and every highlight.js language CodeHighlighter doesn't name. Only the Release build has a feed, so OriCode Molten never updates.

**Data.** SwiftData. `Project { id, name, path, createdAt }`. `Chat { id, project, title, sessionId?, model?, effort?, permissionMode, cwd, createdAt, updatedAt }`, named `Chat` in code only because `Thread` is Foundation's; the interface says thread everywhere. `Event { id, chat, turn, seq, kind, payload: Data, createdAt }`, with `seq` ordering events that share a timestamp, where payload is the JSON of the wire event. Streaming text deltas are coalesced into one `text` event per assistant message, updated in place and shown at most once a frame; never one row per token. If SwiftData fights back on something basic, GRDB is the fallback, decided in the card that hits it.

### Wire protocol

Requests from the app: `{"id": 7, "method": "...", "params": {...}}`. The engine answers `{"id": 7, "result": {...}}` or `{"id": 7, "error": "why"}`. Events from the engine carry no id: `{"event": "...", ...}`.

Methods:

- `hello` → `{ version, models: [{ id, name, description, efforts: [...], fast, defaultEffort, ultra, ultraBlocked }], claude, loggedIn }`. `claude` is the path of the CLI the engine found (null when there is none) and `loggedIn` comes from `claude auth status`, so the app can tell the three failures apart without touching a credential. Models come from the SDK's supported-models call, each named by its version: the name Claude Code's catalog gives the model the row runs (the CLI caches Anthropic's catalog of the account's models in `~/.claude/cache/model-catalog`, and the engine reads the newest `cc` file there and never writes it), or else the version the SDK's line starts with, which the line then loses. The probe runs with CLAUDE_CODE_MODEL_CATALOG=0: Claude Code is moving its list to that catalog behind a flag and serves it only while its copy is fresh, so without the switch the rows and their ids change between launches. After the SDK's rows come any the catalog lists from its main section with a Claude Code version they need and no row runs, with `needs` set to that version, which the app shows dimmed and doesn't pick; then the account's older models, the catalog's overflow rows that no row already runs, each with `more: true` and the levels, Default and fast mode the catalog gives it; the CLI runs them by id. If the call isn't available, a list in `engine/models.ts` marked as a fallback. `defaultEffort`, `ultra` and `ultraBlocked` arrive null, false and null here, and the `models` event that follows fills them in, unless the engine's cache already has them. The engine keeps the SDK's list in the folder the app names in ORICODE_CACHE, for the `claude --version` that gave it, and each model's defaults for the list and the user's settings.json they were read under. A new Claude Code, or no cache, is probed before hello answers; a list a day old is read again in the background a minute after launch, and a `models` event brings what changed.
- `send { threadId, sessionId?, cwd, text, model?, effort?, permissionMode, fast?, attachments?, costSoFar?, grant?, id? }` → `{ ok, waiting? }`. Starts a turn; `sessionId` resumes an earlier one. `effort` is a level or `ultracode`, which goes to the CLI as the session's `ultracode` flag setting instead. `fast` puts `fastMode` in the session's flag settings, the opt-in the CLI asks of SDK sessions. `costSoFar` is what the thread has cost, because a resumed CLI's first result carries the session's saved running total. `grant { tool, input }` is a call the user allowed after a quit had ended the CLI that asked about it: the turn's first ask for the same call, its description aside, is allowed without asking again. `id` is the app's UUID for the message, which the CLI reports on it by. A send while the thread's turn runs, or while messages sent to it still wait, needs one: it goes into the open CLI, which folds it into the turn after the tool that's running returns, or, when the turn has no step left, runs it as a turn of its own as soon as that one ends. It never restarts the CLI or changes the model, level, mode or speed mid-turn, and replies `{ ok, waiting: true }`. A CLI that doesn't report on its messages refuses it, and one that hasn't said yet, before its init, holds it until it has. A send with an `id` and no turn running starts one as usual and is followed by `message.taken` with `newTurn`.
- `interrupt { threadId }` → `{ ok }`. It first takes every message still waiting off the CLI's queue by its id, each with a `message.cancelled`, then interrupts plainly, which leaves the CLI's queued reports of background work alone. It acts between a turn that ended with messages waiting and the start of theirs.
- `setMode { threadId, permissionMode }` → `{ applied }`. `applied` is false when the running turn can't take it and it will hold from the next one.
- `setFast { threadId, fast }` → `{ applied }`, applied to a running CLI with `applyFlagSettings`. `fast.check { threadId, model? }` → `{ state, reason }` asks a probe CLI through its initialize handshake, with no model call, and also sends the answer as a `fast` event naming the model; `threadId` can be one no thread has, since the app keeps the answer per model.
- `answer { requestId, allow, updatedInput?, answers?, message? }` → `{ ok }`. Resolves a pending `ask`.
- `heads.watch { threadId, on }` → `{ ok }`: while on, `heads` carries what each head is doing. `task.stop { threadId, taskId }` → `{ ok }` stops one, through the SDK's stopTask.
- `close { threadId }` → `{ ok }`. Ends the thread's CLI process; the app calls it when a thread is deleted.
- `window { threadId, visible }` → `{ ok }`: the thread open in the window and whether the window can be seen. While it can't, an idle CLI of any other thread is let go at once.
- `git.branch { cwd }` → `{ branch, ahead, upstream }`. Git always runs in the engine, never in the app.
- `git.branches { cwd }` → `{ current, branches: [{ name, current, upstream, track, date, elsewhere }] }`, latest commit first, `elsewhere` the worktree a branch is checked out in when it isn't this one. `git.switch { cwd, branch }`, `git.create { cwd, name, from? }` and `git.previous { cwd }` → the same as `git.branch`; `git.pull { cwd }` → that and `summary`, fast-forward only; `git.remote { cwd }` → `{ web }`, the origin's web page or null. A name is checked with `git check-ref-format --branch` first, and a failure comes back as one plain line that says what to do.
- `git.diff { cwd, since? }` → `{ root, head, mark, files: [{ path, oldPath, status, binary, executable, hunks: [{ oldStart, oldLines, newStart, newLines, context, lines }], added, deleted, cut, stamp }] }`, or `{ root, same: true }` when `since` is the `mark` of the last read and git status, with the size and date of every path it names, has not moved: the working tree against HEAD, or against the empty tree before the first commit, untracked files as new ones, every path from the repository's top whichever folder the thread is in. `status` is M, A, D, R, T or ? for untracked; `cut` leaves out the hunks of a file past 4,000 changed lines or an untracked one past 1 MB, keeping its counts; `stamp` is the size and date of a file shown without lines. The user's diff settings don't reach it: no colour, external diff or textconv, git's own prefixes, histogram, renames found.
- `git.apply { cwd, patch, reverse }` → `{ ok }` takes hunks out of the working tree or puts them back, never the index; git takes all of a patch or none, and a file that has moved on is refused in one plain line. `git.restore { cwd, paths }` → `{ ok }` puts files back as HEAD has them, in the working tree only.
- `git.commit { cwd, paths, message }` → `{ hash }` commits those paths as the working tree has them; `git.commitReviewed { cwd, paths, patch, partial, message }` → `{ hash }` commits whole files for `paths` and the patch's hunks for `partial` through an index of its own, so what's staged by hand stays staged, then gives the real index the new commit's version of what it touched. `git.push { cwd }` → `{ ok }`, and `git.message { cwd, diff }` → `{ message }`, one tool-less Haiku call over the diff the app is about to commit.
- `worktree.add { cwd, slug }` → `{ path, branch }`, `worktree.loss { path, branch }` → `{ dirty, unpushed }`, `worktree.remove { cwd, path, branch }` → `{ ok }`. A worktree thread lives in `.worktrees/<slug>` on branch `oricode/<slug>`.
- `usage` → `{ available, plan, windows: [{ id, label, used, resetsAt }] }`, Claude plan usage from the SDK's usage call through the user's own CLI, kept a minute. `used` is a fraction.
- `commands { threadId?, cwd }` → `{ commands: [{ name, description, hint }] }`, the slash commands and skills the SDK reports for that folder.
- `files.list { cwd }` → `{ files }` (tracked plus untracked, not ignored) and `files.read { cwd, path }` → `{ path, content, truncated }`, read-only, inside the project, cut at 1 MB.
- `shell.run { cwd, command }` → `{ code, line }`, a custom action run quietly: the line through the user's shell in `cwd`, stdin closed, stopped with everything it started after two minutes, `line` its last line of output without colour codes. The app has already single-quoted every value it put in the line.

Events, each with `threadId` except `models`:

- `models { models, settingsEffort, ultraKnown }` after the first `hello`, from the cache or the probes, and again after a daily re-read: the same list with each model's `defaultEffort`, the level a thread that picks none gets (the model's own, or the effortLevel in the user's Claude Code settings); `ultra`, whether the session can run as Ultracode on it; and `ultraBlocked`, `workflows` when dynamic workflows being off is all that stops it. Levels above a maxEffortLevel in the user's settings are left out. `settingsEffort` is that effortLevel, or null. An idle CLI reports all of it through its settings as it's switched from model to model, with no model call. Older models aren't switched to, since the CLI confirms a full model id with a request: their Default is the settings' effortLevel when they have it, else the catalog's, and they have no Ultracode.

- `turn.started { sessionId }`
- `text { delta }`, `thinking { delta }`
- `tool.use { toolUseId, name, input }`
- `tool.result { toolUseId, content, isError, patch? }`. `patch` is the hunks an Edit, MultiEdit or Write applied (`[{ oldStart, newStart, lines }]`, lines prefixed `+`, `-` or a space), from the SDK's structured tool output.
- `ask { requestId, kind: "permission" | "question", tool, input, options? }`. Permission asks come from the SDK's `canUseTool`; `AskUserQuestion` arrives the same way and is answered through `updatedInput`.
- `heads { heads: [{ id, kind, toolUseId, label, type, background, depth, startedAt, tokens?, tools?, step?, line? }] }` whenever the subagents, commands, workflows and other tasks the CLI runs for the thread change, foreground or background: `kind` is `agent`, `command`, `workflow` or `other`, `type` a subagent's type, and `step` `{ tool, detail }`, the call an agent's own frames last made. `tokens`, `tools`, `step` and `line`, a command's last line of output read from the end of its output file, come only while the thread is watched, coalesced into one send in 250ms, and a list the same as the last isn't sent.
- `workflow { taskId, toolUseId, name, state, phases, agents, summary }` when a workflow the thread started begins, with each progress snapshot the CLI sends while it runs, and when it ends: `state` is `running`, `completed`, `failed` or `stopped`, `phases` the titles the CLI has reported, in order, and each agent a `label`, `phase`, `state` (`queued`, `running`, `done` or `failed`), `tokens`, `tools`, `lastTool`, `lastDetail` and `error`. The snapshots ride on the CLI's task_progress messages as `workflow_progress`, which the SDK's types leave out.
- `retrying { attempt, max, error }` while the CLI retries a failed API request, which is what a dropped network looks like.
- `session.lost` when the session a send asked to resume no longer exists; the engine sends the same message again in a new session.
- `ask.cancelled { requestId }` when a pending ask stops waiting (interrupt, or the SDK gave up on it).
- `turn.done { sessionId, stopReason, durationMs, costUSD, usage: { input, output, cacheRead, cacheWrite }, context: { used, window }, waiting }`. `context.used` is the last request's prompt plus output, the number the meter in K-14 draws. `waiting` is how many messages sent during the turn it ended without taking up; each runs as a turn of its own, starting at once.
- `message.taken { messageId, newTurn }` when Claude takes up a message sent with `send` and an `id`: into the running turn, or with `newTurn` as a turn of its own, whose `turn.started` follows.
- `message.cancelled { messageId }` when a waiting message will never run: Stop took it back, the CLI discarded or refused it, or the thread's CLI went away or was closed.
- `compacted { before, after }` when Claude Code compacted the conversation, with the token counts on either side.
- `fast { state, reason, model? }` when fast mode changes for the thread, or when a check answers for the model it names: `state` is `on`, `off` or `cooldown`, and `reason` is the SDK's why-not (`free`, `extra_usage_disabled`, `model_not_allowed` and so on) or null.
- `effort { level, ultracode }` when the level the thread's CLI sends, or whether it runs as Ultracode, is new: read once the CLI is up and after each turn, so a project's settings count. `level` is null for a model without levels, and an Ultracode session reports xhigh.
- `released` when the engine has ended a thread's CLI 90 seconds after its turn, or at once for a thread that isn't open while the window can't be seen (K-160); the thread's next send resumes its session in a new one.
- `limited { resetsAt, window }` in place of an error when one of Claude's plan limits refused the turn: `resetsAt` in milliseconds and `window` the CLI's rateLimitType, `five_hour` for the session limit. A limit the CLI says was reached without a reset comes as an `error` saying it was one of Claude's usage limits, and a refusal with no limit behind it as one saying Claude is limiting requests for a moment.
- `limits { status, rateLimitType, utilization, resetsAt, surpassedThreshold, windows }` when the CLI's rate_limit_event moves a status, a reset or a whole percent: `status` is `allowed`, `allowed_warning` or `rejected`, `rateLimitType` the limit the CLI names, `utilization` and `surpassedThreshold` fractions, `resetsAt` in milliseconds, and `windows` each window's `{ id, used, resetsAt }` from the CLI's undocumented unifiedWindows, empty when it doesn't send them.
- `note { text }` for a notice or warning Claude Code shows in its own transcript, such as the wrap-up it starts near a limit.
- `error { message }`, and without a threadId when the engine itself is in trouble.

Permission modes are the SDK's: `default`, `acceptEdits`, `plan`, `auto`, `bypassPermissions`. The app names them Ask, Accept edits, Plan, Auto, Don't ask.

#### K-165 · The settle and the stream under 8.3ms
What K-164 left. Deltas got 13% slower with K-164's StreamingCodeHighlighter, 4.8ms in Release at one every 10ms, for no hot spot a profile shows: find why, or go back to K-161's version without its 186 background runs a reply. Agents, K-171 to K-194. On 2026-09-26 Meriç asked for more agents beside Claude Code: Codex, Cursor, Grok Build, OpenCode, Antigravity, Copilot, Devin, Pi, Command Code, and the model APIs OpenRouter, DeepSeek, Z.ai and Meta. Three reports stand behind these cards, kept in ~/Library/Developer/OriCode/scratch/providers/: a map of everything Claude-specific in the engine and the app, a probe of the agents installed on this Mac with a sample of each one's events, and a reading of every maker's docs and terms (web-research.md). What was decided with Meriç: an agent that has a CLI comes in through its binary, found on its own like Node is or chosen in Settings, and keeps its own login, which OriCode never reads; Z.ai, DeepSeek, Meta, OpenRouter and Command Code come in by an API key that Meriç types into Settings, kept in the Keychain and handed only to the one process that calls that API; a login a maker forbids for third-party apps (Antigravity with a Google account, pi signed into claude.ai, xAI or Meta) is offered off, and turned on in Settings under the maker's own sentence; each agent's mark is white in the model menu, Claude's staying orange, and an agent's colour shows only on the mark, on its rays and dot; and a thread's head is the president and its rays the workers, which may be any agent (Rays, REA-148). What the probe found on this Mac, which the cards meet: Codex runs on a ChatGPT Go plan, Cursor on its Free plan runs only Auto, Copilot is signed in to a GitHub account with no Copilot plan, OpenCode runs its free models and an OpenRouter key, and Google answers `agy` with "This service has been disabled in this account for violation of Terms of Service"; Grok Build, Devin, Pi, Muse Code and Command Code aren't installed. The shell here exports CLAUDE_CODE_MESSAGING_TOKEN, so every agent's process is started with a scrubbed environment. Order: K-171 first, then K-172 to K-176 one after another, since each moves the same seam; K-177 and K-178 together; K-179 before the agents that speak ACP; the agent cards three at a time once their route is in; K-191 to K-194 last. Every agent card has the same Done when, written once here and not repeated: from the model menu a thread on that agent streams a reply, runs a command after an ask, edits a file with its diff card and the review's provenance, shows its plan when it makes one, stops, and picks up after a quit; its row in Settings › Agents says it's found and signed in, or the Terminal line that would make it so; launch and idle are measured with it turned off and on (rule 6); no test reaches a model; the card's Notes carry its maker's terms, read that day, with URLs; and what couldn't be run on this Mac, an account without a plan or an agent not installed, is said, with what Meriç needs to do to see it.

#### K-171 · Providers: the decision
REA-147. The decision above goes onto the board where it rules. "What this is" and the first line stop saying Claude Code is the one agent. Rule 3, here and in CLAUDE.md, becomes: the app never touches an agent's login, Anthropic's included: no login screen and no token, and each agent runs through its own CLI and the login its user made in Terminal, the app asking the CLI whether it's signed in and never reading a file or keychain item that holds a credential; the one credential OriCode keeps is a model API's key, typed into a native secure field in Settings, kept in the Keychain and handed only to the process that calls that API; a login a maker forbids for third-party apps stays off until it's turned on under the maker's own sentence. The Words paragraph names each agent where it's the subject, as Claude is named now. The Parking lot's "Parallel heads and orchestration" goes, since Rays brings a head's workers in; "What this is not" says OriCode runs nothing you didn't ask for. The Architecture section gains a Providers paragraph for the seam K-172 builds.
Done when: the board and CLAUDE.md say all of that, and REA-147's decision is its first line.

#### K-172 · Claude behind a Provider
The engine only, and nothing changes that anyone can see. engine/provider.ts holds the seam the map drew: a Provider with its id, name, capabilities, levels and modes, found(), availability(), models() and session(), and optional fastCheck, folderCommands, usage and oneShot for git.message; a Session is today's Thread, whose methods are already what every agent needs. The Claude provider is the Thread, the probes in main.ts and claude.ts, models.ts, catalog.ts, cache.ts and usage.ts moved behind that interface with no new logic, every capability true and handoff `claude --resume {session}`. main.ts keeps its method table and a map of sessions by thread, and resolves `provider` only for send, fast.check, commands, usage and git.message; the ask map becomes a registry the Claude session answers from exactly as now. Each provider's `models` event goes out after hello's reply is written, so the setImmediate ordering K-160 leaned on isn't lost. Files don't move, so no test's import breaks.
Done when: every engine test file passes unchanged and tsc is clean; a hello test written before the move passes after it, for Claude missing, signed out and ready from the cache; a Haiku turn traced with a command, an edit, an ask and a Stop gives the same wire lines before and after, ids and times aside; launch still spawns one CLI; make test passes.

#### K-173 · Each thread knows its agent
Chat gains an optional `provider`, nil on every thread from before, which reads as Claude Code's, set with a thread's first message and never after, since a session doesn't move between agents: an optional attribute is a lightweight migration, as colorIndex and resumeAt were. hello gains `providers`, Claude's entry alone for now, with its state, CLI, version, hint, capabilities, levels and modes; the app assumes Claude until hello answers, so nothing flickers. `send` carries `provider` when it isn't Claude's. Defaults that name a model name it with its agent, `codex/gpt-6-luna`, a bare id still meaning Claude's; favorites, fast readings, slash commands and usage are kept by agent. Nothing visible changes.
Done when: copies of OriCode's and OriCode Molten's stores open with every thread on Claude, a thread marked quitMidTurn is still picked up, a unit test covers a defaults key read both ways, and make test passes.

#### K-174 · The engine is ready without a sign-in
Today a signed-out Claude turns off the review, branches, ⌘K's git rows, usage, quit pick-up and limit resume, because they all wait on `engineState == .ready`, which hello's Claude login decides. The engine is ready once hello answers; whether an agent is signed in becomes that agent's own state, and its hint is the line the composer shows in a thread on it. A new `provider.check { provider }` asks one agent again, so Retry after a login in Terminal doesn't restart the engine and stop every turn.
Done when: with Claude signed out the review, branches and ⌘K's git rows work, a Claude thread shows Claude's line, Retry after `claude` logs in makes the thread send without the engine restarting, unit tests cover the states, and make test passes.

#### K-175 · Tools every agent speaks
`tool.use` and `ask` gain an optional `kind` (read, edit, notebook, write, run, search, list, fetch, web, agent, plan, question, planning, skill, workflow, mcp, other) and a `view` of the arguments OriCode reads: path, command, pattern, url, query, description, todos in TodoWrite's shape, and patch. Every agent's edit sends its hunks on `tool.result.patch`, and a plan that isn't a tool call arrives as a `tool.use` of kind plan, so K-169's card and a replay work unchanged. The app keeps Claude Code's names as the table every stored event and every live Claude call without a kind reads through, so the Claude path stays byte-identical, and ToolSummary, Diff, the fold, the ask card, the review's Provenance, the plan card, the heads' step and the quit pick-up switch on the kind. NotebookEdit keeps a kind of its own.
Done when: every Swift test passes unchanged, and stand-in calls of kind run, edit with hunks and plan give Claude's run words, diff counts, provenance and plan card; make test passes.

#### K-176 · What an agent can't do isn't offered
An agent's capabilities, levels and modes from hello decide what the app offers in a thread on it: the effort rail and its levels, fast mode, Ultracode, the permission tiles, sending into a running turn (without it, Return queues, K-148), the heads and their Stop, the limit card and its wait, the usage glass, pick-up after a quit, Continue in, compact, slash commands, Write Message's commit line, and attachments. Words that name the agent come from hello. A stand-in provider with little to offer shows how little.
Done when: Claude's picker, menus and Settings render pixel-identical before and after; a stand-in agent with no levels, modes or steering hides the rail and the tiles and queues on Return; unit tests cover the gates; make test passes.

#### K-177 · Settings › Agents
A pane of native rows, one per agent. For an agent with a CLI: whether it's on, its binary's path, found by the same login-shell lookup Node's is and changeable with Choose… as Node's is, its version, and its state, signed in or the one Terminal line that fixes it (`codex login`, `agent login`, `copilot login`, `opencode auth login`, `grok login`, `devin auth login`, `pi` then /login, `agy`). For a key provider (Z.ai, DeepSeek, Meta, OpenRouter, Command Code): a native SecureField whose key goes to the Keychain as it's saved and is never shown again, only whether one is kept, with Remove. A login a maker forbids for third-party apps is a Toggle, off, under the maker's own sentence, quoted with its link: Google's for Antigravity with a Google account, Anthropic's for pi signed into claude.ai, and xAI's and Meta's for pi's logins with theirs. Only agents turned on are looked for at launch, all of them in the same `zsh -lc`, so launch stays one shell (rule 6). Keys are read from the Keychain only by the engine, for the process it starts, and never logged.
Done when: the pane lists every agent with the right state on this Mac; a key saved goes to the Keychain and survives a relaunch, and Remove takes it out; a forbidden login is off until turned on; launch with every agent on spawns one shell and no agent's CLI; unit tests cover key storage against a test Keychain service and the rows' states; make test passes. Needs Meriç's eye.

#### K-178 · The model menu by agent
The model menu lists models under their agent, each heading with its maker's mark in white and Claude's in orange, Favorites across all of them first; a thread with nothing in it can move to another agent's model, and after its first message the menu offers only its own agent's. Settings › New threads picks the agent new threads start on, and ⌘K's model rows do the same. A model's id is kept with its agent, so two agents' models never collide. The marks are drawn as template images, and README says whose they are.
Done when: frames show the menu with three agents and their marks, a new thread started on Codex from it, and an older thread offered only its own agent's models; make test passes. Needs Meriç's eye.

#### K-179 · ACP in the engine
One provider for every agent that speaks the Agent Client Protocol, v1: it starts the agent's own binary, runs initialize, and calls authenticate only with a method the agent itself runs; session/new, session/load or session/resume, session/prompt and session/cancel; session/request_permission becomes an ask whose choices are the agent's options (allow once, allow always, reject); session/update becomes today's events, message chunks as text, thought chunks as thinking, tool_call and tool_call_update as tool.use and tool.result with ACP's kind mapped to K-175's and a diff as the patch, plan as a plan tool.use, usage_update as the context and cost there is, available_commands_update as slash commands, current_mode_update and config options as the thread's mode and model. fs and terminal requests from the agent are answered only if a later card turns them on. The client is written on the protocol's own messages over the NDJSON the engine already speaks, unless `@agentclientprotocol/sdk` is smaller than what it replaces (rule 6); the card measures and says. One process per thread, released with K-160's idle rule.
Done when: engine tests against a stand-in ACP agent cover the handshake, a prompt with text, thought, a read, an edit with its diff, a permission asked and answered, a plan, a cancel, a load, and an agent answering "Authentication required"; make test passes.

#### K-180 · Codex
Through `codex app-server` over stdio, the user's own codex. initialize names OriCode; thread/start with the thread's folder, model, approval policy and sandbox from its mode; turn/start with the text; item/agentMessage/delta and the reasoning deltas as text and thinking; commandExecution and fileChange items as tool calls of kind run and edit, their diffs as patches; the approval requests as asks with Codex's own decisions; turn/plan/updated as the plan; turn/steer for a message during a turn and turn/interrupt for Stop; thread/resume for pick-up; model/list with its effort levels, low to max, and ultra where a model has it; account/rateLimits as the limits and usage glass; `codex login status` for its state. The server's lines have no "jsonrpc" field and a strict parser would refuse them. Codex keeps OriCode's threads in its own history, which the card's Notes say. Meriç's ChatGPT Go plan runs it here.

#### K-181 · Cursor
Through `cursor-agent acp` on K-179, its Cursor login checked with `cursor-agent status --format json`. Effort, thinking and fast live inside Cursor's model ids, which the menu shows as Cursor names them. It leaves a worker process behind for each folder, which OriCode ends with the thread's session; opening a session takes about 3 seconds, which the composer covers. On the Free plan only Auto runs though the list shows more: the card finds whether Cursor says which, and shows only what runs. Allow always writes into Cursor's own allowlist, as it does in Cursor.

#### K-182 · Copilot
Through `copilot --acp` on K-179. Copilot sends its errors as ordinary text chunks: the card tells them apart, or goes to Copilot's own headless protocol (`--headless --stdio`), which types them and adds cost, quota and a send that joins a turn, whichever is simpler. A GitHub account with no Copilot plan, Meriç's today, shows as its own state, found through the headless auth call, with GitHub's line for getting one.

#### K-183 · OpenCode
Through `opencode acp` on K-179. OpenCode asks for nothing unless told to, so its process is started with OPENCODE_PERMISSION set from the thread's mode. Its models, 392 here, come as provider/model under OpenCode, and the keys a user gave `opencode auth login`, OpenRouter's here, stay OpenCode's. ACP brings no plan from OpenCode: the card either reads its todos from `opencode serve` or leaves the plan out and says so. It loads Claude Code's skills and CLAUDE.md by default, which is left as the user has it.

#### K-184 · Grok Build
Through `grok agent stdio` on K-179, after Meriç installs it (`curl -fsSL https://x.ai/cli/install.sh | bash`) and runs `grok login`; authenticate with its own cached_token method. Grok sends its models on a field and a notification of its own, not as config options, which the card reads.

#### K-185 · Devin
Through `devin acp` on K-179, after Meriç installs it (`brew install --cask devin-cli`) and runs `devin auth login`. The card first finds whether Devin answers a client that isn't Windsurf, and which plans carry the CLI.

#### K-186 · Pi
Through `pi --mode rpc`, the pi Meriç installs (`npm install -g --ignore-scripts @earendil-works/pi-coding-agent`): prompt, steer, follow_up, abort, set_model and get_available_models, and its events as today's. Its models come from the providers pi holds a key for; a model pi reaches through a subscription login another maker forbids (claude.ai, xAI, Meta) is off until turned on in Settings › Agents under that maker's sentence. Pi never asks before a tool, so a thread on it says it runs unsupervised, and offers no permission tiles.

#### K-187 · Antigravity
Through `agy -p` with stream-json both ways, or Google's own ACP server if it's simpler, the agy Meriç has. Its Gemini API key route, set in agy's own settings, is on; its Google-account route is off until turned on under Google's sentence, which the card quotes. Google answers this Mac's account with "disabled … for violation of Terms of Service", so this card is built against the stream's shape and a stand-in, and says it couldn't be run.

#### K-188 · DeepSeek and Z.ai in Claude Code
A thread on DeepSeek or Z.ai is Claude Code, all of it, pointed at the maker's Anthropic-compatible endpoint: its CLI starts with ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL and ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN from the key in the Keychain, and the model names their docs map (deepseek-v4-pro and deepseek-flash, glm-5.3 and glm-5.3-flash). An auth token outranks the subscription login in Claude Code, so Meriç's Claude login is never sent to them: the card proves it from the requests' headers against a local stand-in endpoint. Claude's plan usage, limits and fast mode are hidden on these threads. Anthropic calls non-Claude models through Claude Code unsupported, not forbidden, which the Notes quote.

#### K-189 · OpenRouter and Meta
OpenRouter by its key, through Claude Code's Anthropic skin or through OpenCode with OPENROUTER_API_KEY set for its process, whichever gives the fuller thread, measured on a free model; Meta by its key, through Muse Code's own protocol (`muse serve`, with META_API_KEY for its process) or its Anthropic- or OpenAI-compatible API through an agent that speaks it. The card tries each route, keeps the better, and says why.

#### K-190 · Command Code
`cmd -p --output-format json`, with COMMAND_CODE_API_KEY from the Keychain for its process, the cmd Meriç installs (`npm i -g command-code`), each message resuming the thread's session. Headless it blocks writes and commands unless told otherwise and asks nothing over the wire, so its threads run in the mode the thread sets and say what that allows. The lowest-fidelity agent here, and it says so.

#### K-191 · Each agent's limits, usage and Continue in
Codex's rate limits and Copilot's quota fill the usage glass and the limit card where a thread is on them, each in its maker's words; an agent with none shows no glass. Continue in opens a thread's session in its own CLI in a block, as Continue in Claude Code does: `codex resume`, `cursor-agent --resume`, `copilot --resume`, `opencode -s`, `grok -r`, `devin -r`, with K-155's guards.
Done when: a Codex thread's glass shows its 30-day window, Continue in Codex opens its session in a block and the thread waits for it, and make test passes.

#### K-192 · Heads on different models
REA-149. Each head is drawn in its agent's colour on the mark, the dot for the head and a ray for each worker, Claude's orange and each other agent's own; the palette is chosen on this card from each maker's colour, offscreen, for Meriç to pick from. Colour stays off everything else.
Done when: a thread with a Claude head and three workers, two on Codex and one on Claude, shows an orange dot, two rays in Codex's colour and one orange, in the drawer and at the capsule, and Meriç has picked the palette.

#### K-193 · Rays: a head and its workers
REA-148. The head is the president and its rays the workers. OriCode serves the head a small set of tools of its own over MCP, which Claude Code, Codex and the ACP agents all take at a session's start: start a worker on an agent and model with a task, see how it's doing, read what it brought back, send it a message, stop it. A worker is a session through the same providers as a thread, headless, its asks put to the head's thread; one that edits works in a worktree of its own (K-24), and the head merges what it keeps. ⌘I lists the workers with their agent, model, step, time and cost, the review says which ray changed what, and each worker's cost counts toward the thread's. A thread is started as a pair from the model menu, its head on one agent and its workers allowed on the ones picked, or a head asks for a worker on its own. The Parking lot's line is gone with K-171.
Done when: a Claude head asked to have Codex write a test and Cursor review it starts both, shows them as rays in their colours, reads both results and answers; the Codex worker's edits arrive through its worktree; Stop on the head stops its workers; engine tests cover the tools against stand-in agents; make test passes. Needs Meriç's eye.

#### K-194 · Every agent in the README
README, About and the release notes name every agent, how each comes in, a binary with its own login or a key in the Keychain, the logins that are off until turned on and why, and whose marks the model menu shows; and the 0.2.0 CHANGELOG entry is written for Meriç to release.
Done when: README and About say it, and the CHANGELOG entry waits for Meriç's word.

### Later

Cards written during the freeze, each on Linear too: K-107 is REA-177 and K-116 is REA-176.

#### K-107 · OriCode's own Clear to Tinted
Build this only on Meriç's yes, once they have lived with K-106. It is the slider Meriç named: OriCode's own copy of the system's Liquid Glass slider, whose default is the system's look. Settings › General › Window gets a second native Slider row, Liquid Glass, from Clear to Tinted, with ticks at the system's three notches and Follow System as its default. Away from Follow System, it writes NSGlassTintAmount into OriCode's own defaults and posts NSGlassEffectDiffusionDidChangeNotification. In a probe window that changed the glass live; OriCode itself hasn't been checked. At Follow System it removes the key. Both the key and the notification are private and undocumented. The Architecture section names them and says what happens when a macOS update breaks them: the row stops doing anything and the window stays at the system's look. The row hides when Reduce Transparency or Increase Contrast is on, as the system's own slider does, and on macOS before 27.
Done when: captures of OriCode at Clear and at Tinted differ the way the system's two ends do while a Finder window stays as it was, Follow System puts it back without a relaunch, and the row is hidden with Reduce Transparency on. Needs Meriç's yes before it's built and their eye after.

#### K-116 · Surfaces on the glass
Once the window is Liquid Glass, the things that float over it can be too. The composer, the picker card, the slash menu, the command center, and the review, Files and terminal sheets use `.glassEffect(.regular, in:)` in one GlassEffectContainer, and so does the drawer. The picker card shares a glassEffectID with the composer, so it grows out of the composer's glass. The composer's hand-drawn top highlight goes, and hover and selected fills stay as they are. Each surface takes the same black tint as the window's Glass slider. The brief's Surfaces paragraph is rewritten to match.
Done when: frames show each surface on glass over the window's glass, the picker card rises out of the composer, the terminal's text stays readable, and no surface draws a stroke of its own. Needs Meriç's eye over their wallpaper; if glass on glass reads muddy, this card is reverted and K-106 stays.

### Parking lot

Things that were once on the roadmap and are out on purpose. Each one stays out until there is a reason the official app can't serve.

- Remote control and a phone client: the official app does this.
- Linear or any tracker integration: this file is the tracker.
- Parallel heads and orchestration: Claude Code has subagents and worktrees; see K-24.
- Windows and Linux: the app is the Mac window.
- Light mode: the desktop is the theme.
- Browser and simulator panes: open the official app.
- Custom themes: the glass slider is the theme.

#### K-03 · Engine: the sidecar
`engine/` with `main.ts`, the SDK, and a `tsconfig` used for checking only. Implements the wire protocol above end to end: `hello`, `send` with streaming (`includePartialMessages`), `interrupt`, `setMode`, `answer`, and every event. Tool calls, permission asks and `AskUserQuestion` go through `canUseTool`. `make engine` runs `tsc --noEmit` and `npm ci --omit=dev`.
Done when: from Terminal, `printf '{"id":1,"method":"hello"}\n' | node engine/main.ts` answers with the models, and a `send` for "say hi" against any git repo streams `text` events followed by `turn.done` with a cost. No app involved yet.
Notes: One long-lived SDK query per thread, fed from an open input queue, which is what lets setMode and interrupt reach a running turn. The engine runs the user's own claude (pathToClaudeCodeExecutable) and stages without the SDK's optional 208 MB bundled CLI, so the engine is 47 MB. The wire protocol gained hello's claude and loggedIn, a close method, ask.cancelled, compacted and turn.done's context; the Architecture section says so. make test covers the protocol without starting Claude.

#### K-24 · A thread on its own branch
⌘⇧N: new thread on a new branch in a worktree under `.worktrees/<slug>/`; the thread's cwd is the worktree. Deleting the thread offers to remove the worktree when nothing on it is unpushed, and says what would be lost otherwise.
Done when: two threads edit the same file in parallel and neither sees the other's change until merged.
Notes: A worktree thread gets branch oricode/<slug> in .worktrees/<slug>, which is kept out of the project's own status through .git/info/exclude rather than its .gitignore. What deleting would lose is uncommitted files plus commits no other branch or remote has (rev-list HEAD --not --branches --remotes, excluding its own branch). With nothing to lose, the dialog defaults to removing the worktree and its branch; otherwise it says what would go and defaults to keeping the worktree. Checked: two worktree threads on Haiku told to change greet.swift to "hey" and to "yo" at the same time each saw only their own edit, and the root still returned "hi"; deleting the second said "1 uncommitted file", and Remove took the worktree and branch away. A stale git.branch reply for the project root no longer overwrites the worktree's branch in the capsule.

#### K-33 · Heads light the rays
A thread's heads are its main loop while a turn runs, plus each Agent call that hasn't returned, up to six. The drawer row's state ring becomes a 14pt `RaysMark` with that many rays lit, turning slowly while any are lit; a thread waiting on you pulses its dot. The empty-state mark reads the selected thread the same way.
Done when: a turn that starts two subagents lights three rays on its row, and they go out as the subagents return.
Notes: Counting unfinished Agent calls wasn't enough: Claude sends subagents to the background, the Agent call returns at once, and the turn ends while they work. So the engine tracks the CLI's task messages (task_started, task_notification, task_updated, background_tasks_changed) per thread and emits tasks { running } (Architecture updated), and heads = the main loop while a turn runs + tasks, capped at six. A background agent reporting back makes the CLI start a turn nobody sent; the engine now treats that as a turn, so it gets turn.started, Stop and a footer. Checked: a Haiku turn with two parallel subagents traced tasks running 2, then 1, then 0 as they returned, and one subagent's Bash command waited on a card in the thread like any other ask. RaysMark rendered large showed 0, 1, 3 and 6 rays lit clockwise from twelve; at 14pt in the drawer the agent's zoom can't count rays, so the row itself is for Meriç's eye.

#### K-38 · Usage circle
The circle left of send becomes Claude plan usage. The engine asks the SDK's usage call through the user's own CLI, so no token is read (rule 3), and caches it for a minute; `usage` joins the wire protocol. The ring shows the 5-hour window in kullanym-notch's three bands and sweeps with its reading spring; while the thread runs, a thin white arc turns inside it, as kullanym-notch shows an agent working. Hovering opens a native popover, glass on this macOS: each window as a bar with its reset time in kullanym-notch's wording, and this thread's context as the last bar. The context ring from K-14 retires into that bar.
Done when: the circle shows the session percentage `/usage` shows, and the popover lists the weekly window and the thread's context.
Notes: The engine's usage method spawns the CLI with no settings and asks the SDK's usage call, about a second, and keeps the answer a minute (15 seconds when the app asks fresh after a turn); only the named windows come through, since the response also carries codenamed ones nobody could label (Architecture updated). The app asks when the engine comes up, fresh after each turn, and on hover when its answer is over a minute old. The ring is the session window in kullanym-notch's bands (green under 50%, amber under 70%, red above) and sweeps with its reading spring; while the thread runs, a quarter arc turns inside it once every 1.1s, clock-driven. The card is a native popover that opens a quarter second into a hover and closes 0.3s after the mouse leaves both it and the ring; its bars grow from zero with the reading spring, staggered 45ms, and the reset times use kullanym-notch's wording. Checked: the ring showed 15%, and the card listed Session 15% resetting in 3 hr 37 min, Week 84% resetting Sat 12:00 AM, Fable week 77%, and this thread at 23K of 200K; the arc turned during a turn and the card closed when the mouse left. claude -p /usage, run right after, reported the same three: session 15%, week 84% resetting Sep 26 at 12am, Fable 77%.

#### K-60 · Fast mode
A Fast switch in the picker, for the models the SDK says support it. It belongs to the thread, and new threads start with the last choice, like the model. The engine passes `fastMode` in the session's flag settings, which is the opt-in the CLI asks of SDK sessions, and applies a change to a running thread with `applyFlagSettings`. It forwards `fast_mode_state` and `fast_mode_disabled_reason`, so the switch can say why fast mode isn't on. The Thread menu gets Fast Mode too.
Done when: turning Fast on for an Opus thread shows what the CLI reports: on, or the reason it can't be.
Notes: The SDK marks Default and the two Opus entries as fast-capable, not Fable, Sonnet or Haiku. A probe CLI from a scratch script, which makes no model call, reported fast mode off with sdk_opt_in_required on its own and on once its flag settings carried fastMode: true, so that is what send passes and what setFast applies to a running CLI. Turning Fast on also runs that probe as fast.check, so the switch can say on, or why not, before a turn is spent finding out; turns then keep it current from their init and result messages. Two changes to K-59 came with it: the picker is two columns (models left; effort, speed and permissions right), because one column with Speed added was about 640pt tall and AppKit opened it off to the side, and the effort segments share one width, so Extra high reads X-high there. Checked: with Default picked, Thread › Fast Mode put a bolt on the model button and the picker read Fast mode, On for this model, from the CLI's check; turning it off and going back to Haiku left the thread as it was. No fast turn was run, to keep plan usage out of the check. make test passes on both suites.

#### K-71 · Animations at the rate they need
The turning rays, the waiting pulse and the usage circle's working arc redraw through TimelineView at the display's rate, up to 120 times a second. They're capped at 30 frames a second, and 60 for the arc, which turns once a second.
Done when: the app's CPU while a turn runs is lower than before, with the numbers in the notes.
Notes: The caps did nothing: measured in the Debug build with a Haiku turn waiting on an ask (the arc turning, a ray lit and turning in the pinned drawer, the dot pulsing, nothing streaming), OriCode used 10.2% of a core before and 10.7% after. A sample showed why: each frame of a TimelineView made SwiftUI lay the whole window out again, and the arc alone cost about 10%. So nothing in the app animates through TimelineView now. The arc is a shape layer, and the mark, while it turns or waits, is its six rays and dot as layers, the paths from the same Ray shape; Core Animation turns and pulses them in the render server. The still mark stays SwiftUI, which the icon renderer draws too. The drawer's marks also stop while the drawer is hidden, since it stays in the tree. Checked in the same waiting state: 0.0% CPU with no idle wakeups over twelve seconds, with the drawer hidden and with it shown; two frames half a second apart showed the rays turned about 20° with the dot dimmed, and the arc gone from seven o'clock to four.

#### K-76 · Default lands somewhere
Default effort sends no level, so Claude Code picks one: the model's own, or the effortLevel in the user's Claude Code settings. Nothing said which, so choosing Default looked like choosing nothing. The engine reads each model's default from an idle CLI with the user's settings and no model call, along with whether the model can run Ultracode, why not when only workflows are off, and any effort cap, and sends them after hello. Each thread's own CLI then reports the level it runs at, so a project's settings count too. The model button names the level Default lands on, fainter than a level picked, and the Thread menu says Default (Medium) and lists Ultracode above Max. Settings › New threads gets a menu each for model, effort, fast mode and permissions, fixed to one choice or left on Last picked, which is what all four do today. With no thread open, a pick in the composer starts the thread it's for, so a fixed default can't overrule it. Ultracode never carries over to a new thread.
Done when: on Meriç's settings an Opus thread on Default reads Opus Medium and the Thread menu's Effort says Default (Medium) with Ultracode listed; with Sonnet and High fixed in Settings, ⌘N starts a Sonnet thread on High while the last pick was Haiku; make test covers the engine's readings on a stubbed CLI.
Notes: The engine asks an idle CLI with the user's own settings, and their hooks off, for each model's default through the SDK's get_settings, which it has as a control request but not yet as a typed method; there's no model call. On Meriç's Mac every model with levels defaults to Medium, because ~/.claude/settings.json sets effortLevel to medium, not the High the models ship with. Whether a model can run as Ultracode is read from a second idle CLI started with ultracode in its settings, since switching it on in a running CLI makes Claude Code save its launch-effort unpins into ~/.claude.json for good; that was caught in review before it shipped, and Meriç's file already had them from before this work. An effort cap in the user's settings takes the levels above it off, and a model kept from Ultracode only by workflows being off says so. Each thread's CLI reports the level it runs at once it's up and after every turn, with the effort it was started with, so a reading taken on Default, project settings included, moves Default's place for that thread and model. The engine's tests cover the readings on stubbed CLIs. With no thread open, a pick that changes nothing no longer starts a thread, a thread with no model of its own shows Claude Code's default model rather than whatever the next thread would start on, and a send only carries a level the model still has. Checked: an Opus thread on Default reads Opus Medium with Medium faint, the Thread menu's Effort reads Default (Medium) and ends with Ultracode, and with Sonnet and High fixed in Settings, ⌘N started a Sonnet thread on High while the last pick was Haiku; make test passes 15 engine tests.

#### K-82 · Heat at the top of the scale
The top of the rail only drifted motes, and Max and Ultracode looked alike apart from the rays in the thumb; Meriç asked for more. Now the heat goes somewhere. At Max, slugs of light run along the fill and sink into the thumb with the glow round it breathing in as each lands, embers lift off the fill's hot half, a jet of sparks flies off the rim, born white-hot and cooling to Claude's orange, and the fill's last stretch burns paler toward the thumb. Arriving throws embers back out of the thumb that slow and are drawn in again, with a flash of the glow and a spit of sparks. At Ultracode the same heat has six places to go: a wheel of six jets turns with the rays, embers lift off the whole fill, three quicker slugs land at uneven beats, the halo widens, and arriving lights the six jets one after another. When the few seconds of motion end, the rays coast on to where the next ray stands instead of stopping where they are, so the mark rests upright, and the wheel with them. A pointer moving along the rail at the top keeps the heat up and a still one lets it rest. What's thrown off stays about the rail, under a mask that keeps it off the text. The brief's Ink and Motion lines say so.
Done when: frames at Max and Ultracode show the slugs, embers, sparks and the arrival, and at rest the hot core and an upright mark with nothing moving; OriCode reads 0.0% CPU with the picker open.
Notes: Picked from four designs (heat, many heads, craft, beyond the rail) by two judges, one for delight and one for craft; both chose heat and grafted the others' settle, mote shape and arrival fixes onto it. EffortEffects is now one view reaching 20pt above and below the rail and 40pt past the thumb's centre, placed with .position so the rail's hit area doesn't grow, under a vertical mask clear from 18pt above the rail; the fill keeps its own clip and 10pt fade inside it for the motes, burst, streaks and slugs. Max: motes grow from about 1pt to 4pt and fade to nothing instead of vanishing at a quarter; two slugs land at 1.5s and 3.3s, each with an additive breath of a radial glow round the thumb; embers leave the fill's hot half at 8 a second; a jet at half past ten on the rim throws 11 sparks a second, white-hot cooling to #D97757; the fill's last 56pt run 40% of the way to ember. Arriving throws about 28 embers back at 95pt/s against 190pt/s² toward the thumb, flashes the glow and spits 8 sparks. Ultracode: six jets on a wheel turning with the rays, 13 to 15 sparks a second each with their own seeds; embers off the whole fill at twice Max's rate; three slugs at uneven beats; the last 96pt run 55% toward ember and the halo widens from 9 to 11pt; arriving puffs 10 sparks from each jet 40ms apart. Arrivals now also come by arrow key, and wake the rail when they happen rather than on release. When the rail rests, the thumb's rays and the wheel coast for a second on to the next multiple of 60°, so the mark stands upright; the drawer's marks still stop where they are. A pointer moving onto another stop at the top keeps the rail awake without sending the slugs again, and the slugs hold while fast mode's streaks run the other way. The design's sizes left the wheel too faint to see in frames, so its sparks went from 5 to 14 a second and from 1.6 to 2.4pt, and Max's jet from 7 to 11. Checked with frames: arriving at Max by key shows the thrown embers, the spit and the flash, then slugs gliding in among motes and embers; Ultracode shows the wheel's spiral arms turning with the rays and embers along the whole rail; at rest nothing moves, the hot core shows and the mark stands upright. OriCode read 0.0% CPU while it all moved, with one 6.3% sample at the key press. WindowServer read 38 to 40% with the picker closed, open at rest and moving alike, so something else on screen held it up and the picker's own share couldn't be read this time. The hover wake wasn't checked.
