#### K-84 · Every level lit
Below Max the rail looked unfinished. Claude's orange thinned to between 30 and 56% mixed with whatever was behind the glass and read brown, Low's whole fill sat under the thumb so it showed no orange at all, the stops on the fill were black specks and the thumb was the same grey knob at every level. Meriç said the picker was dragging the app back. The fill becomes Claude's orange at full strength at every level, lit like a tube, paling toward ember over a stretch before the thumb that grows with the level. Low's stop moves in so a cap of fill always shows. Stops the fill has passed light as ember lamps, and the pour lights them in order as the picker opens. The thumb becomes a bead lit from below by its level's heat, white only at the top of the scale, with a glow that reaches further at each level, and the level's name takes its colour. Back to Defaults' ghost becomes a faint disc, and Auto's icon stops using sparkles, which belong to the rail now. The brief's Ink line says so.
Done when: frames at Low, Medium, High, Extra high, Max and Ultracode show solid orange with lit lamps, Low's cap, each level's bead and the title in the level's colour.
Notes: Chosen from a study of T3 Code's picker (a radio menu that closes on one click) and Claude Code's own /effort slider (a text rail whose colour and motion climb from Low to Max, with Ultracode past a divider), then three designs (Heat curve, Lens and Lamps, Filament) judged for delight and for craft; the delight judge chose Heat curve and the craft judge Filament, and both found the same cause: orange at 30 to 56% over the glass reads brown, and Low's fill sat under the thumb. The build takes Filament's colours, which are only Claude's orange and Ink.ember, over Heat curve's new reds, and Heat curve's bead lit from below without a highlight spot, which its renders showed reads as an eye round a black dot. EffortScale.burn gives each level its core length, heat toward ember, whiteness and glow: Low 0pt, 0.06, 0.45, 20% at 5pt; Medium 22pt, 0.12, 0.5, 26% at 6pt; High 32pt, 0.2, 0.56, 32% at 7pt; Extra high 44pt, 0.3, 0.63, 40% at 8pt; Max 56pt, 0.4, 0.8, 60% at 9pt; Ultracode 96pt, 0.55, 0.84, 60% at 11pt. The fill is three stops so SwiftUI animates between levels, with a white 14% to clear to black 12% shading and a 1pt white 28% line 2pt under its top; its colour change now rides the thumb's own spring rather than a separate fade. EffortTrack starts 13pt further in (EffortRail.cap), so Low shows a 13pt cap. Lamps are 4.5pt ember 90% with a 2pt ember glow, the Default ring on the fill is ember, and the pour lights them 0.05s plus 0.4s times their share of the way. The bead mixes the level's heat with white from 0.75 of the way at the top to the level's whiteness at the bottom, with a 1pt rim from white 60% to 8%; the title is Claude's orange paled by 1.25 times the level's heat, and white under Increase Contrast. The ghost is a white 12% disc, and Auto is checkmark.shield. Checked with frames: Low shows its cap and a rose bead, Medium a lit Low lamp and a peach bead, High and Extra high the lamps and the ring lit in ember with a paler end, Max and Ultracode the 0.0.83 heat on the new fill with a near-white bead, and each title in its level's colour.

#### K-85 · Every level arrives
Moving to a level below Max changed a word and nothing else. Now light runs up the fill into the bead when the level rises, sized to the level: a slug at Medium, a brighter one and a few embers at High, a sweep and lingering motes at Extra high, and the lamps it passes flare. When the level falls, the heat drains back into the bead and the lamps go out behind it. Held arrow keys play one arrival for the level they stop on. Below Extra high a change moves for under a second. The brief's Motion line says so.
Done when: frames catch an arrival at Medium, High and Extra high and the cooling on the way down, nothing moves in the still frame after each, and OriCode reads 0.0% CPU with the picker open.
Notes: EffortEffects now exists at every level. It spans the whole rail and reaches past its end, and takes the thumb's position as animatable data, so every step of the thumb's spring reaches it and the heat a fall gives up stays inside it. The rail passes it the stops' positions, the stop under the thumb and an arrival: bumped 180ms after a run of changes stops, with the stop the run began from, so a held key crossing three levels arrives once. Rising: a slug of 30 by 13pt at 40% over 0.5s at Medium, 34 by 14pt at 48% over 0.48s at High, and a 26 by 22pt band at 80% over 0.42s at Extra high, each on an ease-in from the rail's start into the fade before the bead. Each lit lamp flares, a 12pt ember sprite scaling 0.5 to 1.3 to 1 over 0.32s, when the carrier reaches it (the travel time times the square root of its share of the way). The glow breathes as it lands, and High and Extra high throw 4 and 8 embers from a puff emitter at the hot end; Extra high keeps motes at 4 a second for the live window. Falling by key, click or Delete: a capsule of Claude's orange from 55% at the bead to 30% at the old thumb shrinks into the bead over 0.45s and fades over 0.5s; a fall a drag made plays only the bead's dip. The bead pulses to 1.05 for each stop a drag crosses and dips to 0.95 on a fall. Max's first slug and each of Ultracode's now flare the lamps, and below Max the picker's opening breathes the glow once. While fast mode's streaks run, the carrier and flares hold and the breath and embers stay. Checked with frames: four lefts from Ultracode to Medium drain once, from the old thumb into the bead; a right to High sends a slug and flares the Low lamp; a right to Extra high sweeps, flares the lamps one after another and throws embers; two lefts back to Medium drain again. At rest nothing moved and OriCode read 0.0% CPU with the picker open. WindowServer read 40% at rest, as it did with the picker closed during 0.0.83's check, so the picker's share still can't be read on this machine today. A stash used to split the plan from K-84 left three iCloud copies of the changed files in App/, which XcodeGen compiled twice; they were identical to the committed files and were moved out.

#### K-87 · Three pickers to choose from
Meriç didn't like the picker as a whole and asked for a few designs built from the ground up, to choose one. All three open as a card of the composer's own glass rising out of its right end, the way the slash menu rises out of its left, instead of a system popover with a material and an arrow of its own; a click anywhere else, Esc or Return puts it away. A · Slider keeps the reference's structure: the level over the model, one slider with a frosted glass thumb, the permission modes as tiles. B · Mark makes OriCode's mark the control: the dot, the main head, grows and heats from a small white point at Low to Claude's orange at Max as you drag across it or press the arrows, and at Ultracode the six rays light around it as heads. C · Columns lays model, effort and permissions out as three columns of words, a highlight gliding to each choice and a small meter on every level. Thread › Picker Design switches between them until one is chosen; the other two go then.
Done when: each of the three opens from the model button and from ⌘⇧M, sets the thread's model, effort, fast mode and permission mode, and closes on a click outside; frames show all three.
Notes: PickerCard draws the picker as an overlay of the Composer, bottom-trailing and 10pt above it, in Surface.composer over ultraThinMaterial with the composer's top highlight and a black 28% shadow; it rises on Motion.glide from 0.92 and 8pt down and leaves on a fade. A local mouse-down monitor puts it away for a click outside it that isn't on the model button (whose frame AppModel now keeps), and focus goes back to the composer's field when it closes. ModelMenu lost its popover and the room-above check that picked the compact one. A is the old page in the card at 320 by 208: the rail without its Default label (a tag by the title says it), the line under the rail always shown, and a frosted thumb of ultraThinMaterial under the level's bead colour at 55%. B is PickerMark.swift at 320 by 300: an 88pt RaysMark with its rays at 13% until Ultracode lights them clockwise and turns them for four seconds, a dot of 16, 20, 24, 28, 32 and 28pt from Low to Ultracode going from white through ember to Claude's orange with a glow to match and a pop on each change; a drag across the mark steps a level every 26pt with the trackpad's detents and needs 22pt more to pass into Ultracode, and a row of dots under the name picks directly, the chosen one a gliding capsule. C is PickerColumns.swift at 500 by 252: Model, Effort and Permissions columns of 25pt rows with one gliding Surface.selected highlight each, a five-bar meter per level (Max's in Claude's orange) filling bar by bar as it's chosen, Ultracode 4pt apart under the rays, the hovered row's line at the foot beside Fast and Back to Defaults; left and right move between columns and up and down choose. Thread › Picker Design holds the choice in pickerDesign. Checked with frames: A in the card at Medium; B from Low through Max, the dot growing and heating, and into Ultracode with the rays lighting; C with Opus, Ultracode and Ask highlighted, then Medium chosen by a click, and a click on the transcript closing the card.

Meriç chose B, the mark over the slider, so A and C go, with Thread › Picker Design. Once clicked, the bolt lights up white on a lit circle with a glow, faint when it's off, struck through when the CLI turns fast mode down. While it's on, the slider's streaks run for a few seconds and then stand still behind the thumb; the mark's head trails speed lines, darts forward as fast comes on, and streaks run through the mark for those same seconds. FastButton: on (asked and not turned down or paused) is a semibold bolt.fill in pure white on white 20% with a white 35% glow at 8pt; off is the outline bolt in white 30%, 55% on hover; paused by a rate limit keeps the filled bolt in secondary on white 8%; refused is bolt.slash. PickerState.fastOn counts fast as on while the CLI checks as well as once it serves it, and B passes that to the slider, whose streaks and ignite sweep now start with the click; when the four seconds end, three still streaks fade in behind the thumb. The mark: three ember speed lines of 10, 16 and 10pt trail 4pt behind the dot, sliding in with the switch; the dot darts 8pt forward stretched to 1.35 and springs back as fast comes on; and a Core Animation emitter of the slider's streaks runs through a 150 by 80pt band round the mark, faded at both ends, for the live window. Checked with frames: on a thread with fast on, the lit bolt, the speed lines, the streaks through the mark and along the slider while the CLI checked; then the CLI's answer on this account, 'Needs usage credits on your Claude account', with the bolt struck through and the effects gone.

#### K-93 · Fast mode says the same thing every time
Meriç found the bolt and the effects sometimes worked and sometimes didn't. On this account Claude Code turns fast mode down ("Needs usage credits on your Claude account"), and the app kept that answer per thread and only after asking: the first time a thread turned fast on it lit up for a second before the answer came, after that the same thread showed the refusal at once, and a thread that hadn't asked showed fast as on. The answer belongs to the account and the model, not the thread, so the app keeps it per model and asks as the picker opens; the bolt and the effects then say what Claude Code will do, the same way every time.
Done when: on this account, opening the picker on a thread with fast on settles on the struck bolt and the reason, turning fast off and on again shows the refusal straight away with no effects, and a thread that hasn't asked shows the same.
Notes: AppModel.fastReadings holds the last answer per model id: every fast event updates it, a check's for the model it names (the engine now puts model in the event) and a thread's own CLI's for the model the thread is on. PickerState, the Fast button, the line under the level and the composer's bolt read it through fastReading(for:), and the thread's own fastState and fastReason are gone. The picker asks for its model as it opens when there's no answer yet, with a threadId no thread has, so the answer is usually in before the bolt is clicked; turning fast on still asks again, which is how credits added later would show. Checked with frames on this account: a thread with fast on showed Checking fast mode for a moment after launch, then the struck bolt and 'Needs usage credits on your Claude account'; turning fast off gave the outline bolt and the level's own line, and on again the refusal at once with no streaks or speed lines.

#### K-97 · Models by their version
The picker names models by family: Opus, Fable, Sonnet, Haiku. Name them by version the way Claude Code's own catalog does (Opus 5, Fable 5.1, Sonnet 5, Haiku 4.5), in the models page, the picker's model line, the composer button and the Thread menu. The version comes from the catalog Claude Code keeps of the account's models, matched on the model each row resolves to, or else from the start of the SDK's line about the model; Default stays Default, with its line saying which model it runs. The line under a name no longer repeats the version.
Done when: the models page lists Default, Opus 5, Fable 5.1, Sonnet 5 and Haiku 4.5 with this Mac's Claude Code, and the composer button says Opus 5 on an Opus thread.
Notes: engine/catalog.ts reads the newest *-cc.json in the CLI's model-catalog cache (CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR or ~/.claude), keeping rows with a string id and name that aren't disabled or deprecated, and treats a missing or unreadable cache as no catalog. fromSDK takes that list: versioned() names a row by the catalog entry for its resolvedModel without [1m], or by the start of its line when that starts with the row's family, and strips the name and a following 'with' or '·' from the line; Default keeps 'Default (recommended)' and its line. The app shows the names as they come, so the models page, the model line, the composer button, the Thread menu and 'has one reasoning level' all carry the version. Checked: make test's engine suite passes with six new tests (the catalog's names, the line's names, the catalog over the line, a custom row left alone, the newest cc file over an older one, the desktop's ccd file, a broken file and retired rows, and no cache); on the Debug build the composer said Opus 5, the models page listed Default (recommended), Opus 5 with '1M context · Best for everyday, complex tasks', Fable 5.1, Sonnet 5 and Haiku 4.5, and Thread › Model listed the same.

#### K-98 · More models
People keep using older models because they like them. Claude Code's catalog lists the account's older models in an overflow section (today Opus 5, Fable 5, Opus 4.8, Opus 4.7, Opus 4.6 and Sonnet 4.6), and the CLI runs any of them by id, but the SDK's list leaves them out. The engine adds them from the catalog, each with its levels and fast mode, skipping one a main row already runs, and the models page shows them under a More models heading, scrolling when the page runs taller than the effort page.
Done when: the models page shows More models with Opus 4.8 among them, and a Haiku-sized test turn on one of them (Sonnet 4.6) answers, with the thread's model line saying Sonnet 4.6.
Notes: catalog.ts now reads each row's section, effort options and their Default badge, fast_mode, thinking type and min_claude_code_version. older() turns the overflow rows into models with more: true, leaving out one whose id a row's resolvedModel already runs and one naming a Claude Code version, and adds those that think with effort to the adaptive set; the three older models checked through a probe (Opus 4.6, Sonnet 4.6, Fable 5) all report adaptive thinking and the catalog's levels. They go after the SDK's rows in hello. learnDefaults switches the idle CLIs through the SDK's rows only, and settled() gives an older model the settings' effortLevel as its Default when it has that level, else the catalog's: switching to a full model id costs a confirming request each. The app decodes more, ModelsPage draws its rows in groups with a faint More models heading, the page is as tall as its rows up to the effort page's 308pt and scrolls past that, the arrow keys walk the rows as drawn, and a row with no line (older rows have none) shows its name alone; Thread › Model and Settings › New threads put the older models after a divider. The catalog gives Opus 4.6 fast mode where this CLI's own list doesn't, so its bolt may meet a standard-speed line. Checked: make test's engine suite passes with three more tests (older rows, the Opus a newer one replaces, Default from the settings); on the Debug build the models page showed Default, Opus 5, Fable 5.1, Sonnet 5 and Haiku 4.5, then More models with Fable 5, Opus 4.8, Opus 4.7, Opus 4.6 and Sonnet 4.6 below the fold; nine down arrows walked to Sonnet 4.6 through the heading and Return picked it, which gave the composer 'Sonnet 4.6 Medium' and four stops with Medium as Default; 'Reply with the single word ok.' answered ok, and the session's log records claude-sonnet-4-6.

#### K-99 · Favourite models first
Star a model and it sits at the top of the models page under Favourites, in the order starred; unstar it and it goes back to its place. A star shows on a row under the pointer and stays on a favourite, and each row's context menu has Add to Favourites or Remove from Favourites. Favourites are kept in the app's defaults by model id, and one Claude Code stops listing is left out without being forgotten.
Done when: starring Opus 4.8 and Sonnet 4.6 puts them at the top under Favourites, the arrow keys walk the rows in the order drawn, and after a relaunch they are still there.
Notes: AppModel.favoriteModels keeps the starred ids in the order starred under the defaults key favoriteModels, and toggleFavorite adds or drops one. ModelsPage.groups makes Favorites (those still listed, in that order), then Claude Code's own models, headed Models once there are favorites, then More models, leaving out an empty group; AppModel.modelGroups is what the page, its height, its arrow keys, Thread › Model and Settings › New threads all draw, with a divider between groups in the menus. The UI says Favorites, the Mac's spelling. The star is a button of its own beside the row's button, 18pt plus the row's end padding, shown on the row under the pointer or the arrow keys and on a favorite, filled in secondary ink on a favorite and outlined in faint ink otherwise; laid over the row as an overlay it was a child of the row's button, and an accessibility press on it picked the model instead. Each row also has Add to Favorites or Remove from Favorites in its context menu and as a named accessibility action. A group's first row scrolls its heading into view with it, so arrowing up to the top shows Favorites. #### K-104 · Fast, bubbles in the head
Meriç wants fast mode to show in the mark itself instead of any of K-100's three looks. With fast on, bubbles swirl inside the head at every level. They are ring bubbles on three orbits, the inner ones quicker like a whirlpool. They are Claude's orange at 60% on the white dots from Low to High and white-hot on the orange of Extra high, Max and Ultracode, clipped to the dot and growing with it on the same spring. The layer is a new FastBubbles NSViewRepresentable in PickerMark.swift that never takes the scrub's mouse-down. At Ultracode, the head's rays, the thumb's rays and EffortEffects' jet wheel turn once in half a second instead of nine. Each ray trails nine copies of itself drawn a moment earlier, from a CAReplicatorLayer with instanceDelay wrapped round RaysView's spinner; Core Image's motion and zoom blurs go the wrong way for a spin. They coast to rest upright over 1.2 seconds, with the coast backdated by the trail's length so the trail doesn't drop out. Both effects run while the mark is live, and switching fast now makes it live too. Then they stop where they are, and the bubbles stay drawn while fast is on. FastLook, FastTag, the bolt, the echoes and Thread › Fast Look go, including the Tag look Meriç had as the default. The brief's Motion, Layout and Ink paragraphs gain the fast spin and the bubbles.
Done when: with fast on, frames at Low, Medium, High, Extra high, Max and Ultracode show bubbles in the head. They swirl within 4 seconds of opening the picker, of a level change and of switching fast, and are still afterwards. At Ultracode the mark's and the thumb's rays go round in half a second with a trail, the jets stay on their rays, and the rays coast upright. With Reduce Motion nothing turns and the bubbles show still. Fast off takes the bubbles out and leaves the nine-second turn. Thread › Fast Look is gone and `make test` passes. Needs Meriç's eye for how the blur reads at speed, and whether the bubbles should keep swirling the whole time the picker is open.
Notes: RaysMark has fast, and RaysView wraps its spinner in a CAReplicatorLayer: one instance at rest, nine in a fast turn, each drawn 1/480s earlier and 0.11 fainter, so the trail is the turn's own earlier frames. startTurning takes a period (9s, or 0.5s fast at up to 120fps) and restarts from the presentation angle when the speed changes; coastToRay takes the period and the trail's span, reaches at least speed times duration over 4.8 on (about 180 degrees at speed) so it leaves at the turn's own speed, starts the span early so the copies don't drop out, and drops to one copy when it lands unless a newer turn began. EffortEffects turns its Ultracode wheel at the same period and the thumb's rays get fast too, so the jets stay on their rays; switching fast at Ultracode stokes the rail. FastBubbles is a layer view over the head: a 32pt disc clipped to a circle and scaled to the level's size with the head's spring, holding three orbits (one bubble at 3.2pt every 0.6s, two at 9pt every 0.9s, three at 11.8pt every 1.4s) of ring bubbles, a 1.6pt stroke over a 22% fill, that fizz while they swirl; they swirl while the mark is live, coast to a stop over 0.8s leaving at each orbit's speed, and stay drawn, and a covered window or Reduce Motion stops them on the spot. Heat gives the bubbles Claude's orange at 60% on the white heads (65% at High) and white-hot at 95% on the orange ones. Switching fast wakes the mark. FastLook, FastTag, the bolt, the echoes and Thread › Fast Look went. Checked with frames on an Opus 5 test thread: orange ring bubbles in the white head at Low; white-hot bubbles in the orange head at Max; at Ultracode the six lit rays smeared into a turning blur on two frames a moment apart with bubbles swirling, and six seconds later the rays rested upright with no trail and the bubbles still drawn; fast off took the bubbles out and the rays went back to their slow turn. Medium, High and Extra high weren't framed, and Reduce Motion wasn't tried, since that's a system setting; make test passes. Needs Meriç's eye on how the blur reads at speed and whether the bubbles should keep swirling while the picker is open.

#### K-108 · ⌘K has levels
Meriç wants ⌘K to be the app's brain: everything the app does, reachable from one place. Go To becomes the command center, on the same glass card with the same native List and TextField, so rule 1 gains nothing. App/Palette.swift holds PaletteItem, PaletteAction (run, task, list, input, settings) and PaletteState, a stack of levels. A row can open a sub-list, or a level that asks for text in the field itself, with a live hint line under it. Esc, or ⌫ in an empty field, goes back one level, and Esc at the top closes it; that is handled in EscapeStack.escape(), because its monitor sees Esc before the field does. With nothing typed, the list has sections: Now (Stop, Compact near the context limit, Send again after an error), Recent, Threads and Commands. Once something is typed, the list is flat and the leaves of the sub-lists show among the results, named like "Model: Opus 5" or "Permissions: Plan". This card adds the Model, Effort, Permissions, Switch project and Settings-pane sub-lists. It also adds the thread commands: Copy last reply, Copy thread as Markdown, Copy session ID, Send the last message again, Pin, Rename, Delete, Close, and Next and Previous thread on ⌃Tab and ⌃⇧Tab. The menu item becomes Command Center….
Done when: ⌘K › Model › a model changes the composer's model. Esc steps back one level and then closes the palette. Typing "plan" finds Permissions: Plan, ⌃Tab opens the next thread, and Tests/PaletteTests.swift passes under `make test`.
Notes: App/Palette.swift holds PaletteItem (command, thread, project or choice, with a line, search words, a shortcut, a tick for the current choice and a reason when it can't run), PaletteAction (run, task, list, input), PaletteList, PaletteInput with its hint, PaletteState's stack of levels, and Palette.rank: fuzzy over the title and words, plus 2 points a place for the last twenty rows used, minus 12 for a choice from inside a list, commands first on a tie. App/PaletteCommands.swift builds the rows and runs them, and App/CommandCenter.swift, which replaces GoTo.swift, draws them: the level's name before the field, one line under it for what's running, what went wrong or the hint, and the rows in a native List with headings, scrolling past about twelve. With nothing typed the top level shows Now (Stop, Compact past 70% of the context, Send the last message again after a failed send), Recent, the six latest threads and every command that can run; typed, it's one ranked list of commands (those that can't run faint, with why), threads, projects and 'Model: Opus 5', 'Effort: High', 'Permissions: Plan' and 'Settings: Notifications'. The commands this card adds: Stop, Compact, Send the last message again, Copy last reply, Copy thread as Markdown, Copy session ID, Pin, Rename, Delete, Close, Next and Previous thread (⌃Tab, ⌃⇧Tab, in the Thread menu too), Show threads, Model…, Effort…, Permissions…, Fast mode on or off, Back to defaults, Star the model, Model and effort, Switch project…, Add project, Find a file, Changes, Settings and Keyboard shortcuts. Esc goes back one level and closes at the top through escape(); Tab opens a row with a level; ⌫ in an empty field goes back. Settings keeps its pane in the defaults key settingsPane so a Settings row opens on its pane. View › Command Center… is ⌘K, and the title capsule and the shortcuts sheet say so. Checked on the Debug build: the top level showed Threads and Commands; 'model' found Model…, every 'Model:' choice and a faint Opus 5.5 with 'Needs Claude Code 2.1.280'; Model… opened its level, and Sonnet 5 there turned the composer to Sonnet 5; reopened, Recent held Model: Sonnet 5 and Model…; 'plan' found Permissions: Plan; Thread › Next Thread opened the next thread with the drawer's peek; PaletteTests pass in make test. Not checked, since the agent's arrow, Return, Esc and ⌫ keys don't reach a text field: the keys in the palette, which Meriç's hands will tell.

#### K-109 · Branches in ⌘K
Changing and creating branches belongs in ⌘K, and git stays in the engine. The engine gains git.branches, git.switch, git.create, git.previous, git.pull and git.remote. Each checks a name with `git check-ref-format --branch` first, and turns git's errors into one plain line, such as "Uncommitted changes to f would be overwritten. Commit or stash them first." engine/test/git.test.ts tests them against a scratch repository and never calls Claude. The palette gains Switch branch… (⌘⇧B, also in the Thread menu), whose list offers "Create branch <name>" when nothing matches. It also gains Create branch… with a live hint, Previous branch, Pull, Push, Copy branch name, and Open on GitHub. In a worktree thread these are unavailable, with "This thread's branch is its own", and so they are while Claude is working in that folder. After a switch, every thread on that folder shows the new branch.
Done when: Create branch "try me" makes try-me and the capsule shows it, and Switch branch goes back. A conflicting dirty switch shows its one line inside the palette, Switch branch is unavailable in a worktree thread with its reason, and the engine suite passes.
Notes: engine/git.ts gained branches (for-each-ref with NUL-separated fields, the worktree path compared with the repository's own), switchTo, create, previous, pull (fast-forward only, counting what came), remote and webURL, and friendly, which turns an overwritten-by-checkout list, a branch used by another worktree, an invalid reference, split histories and a missing upstream into one line each and otherwise keeps git's first line without its fatal: or error:. Every name goes through check-ref-format --branch, whose output has to be the name itself, so @{-1} is refused too. engine/test/git.test.ts runs them on scratch repositories with the user's git config shut out (GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL=/dev/null). App/PaletteGit.swift puts Switch branch… (⌘⇧B, also Thread › Switch Branch…), Create branch…, Previous branch, Pull, Push, Copy branch name and Open on GitHub in ⌘K. They run in the thread's folder and are unavailable, with why, while the engine is down, in a worktree thread ('This thread's branch is its own') and while Claude is working in that folder. Switch branch lists the branches with upstream and track, ticks the current one, turns a branch checked out in another worktree into a way to its thread (paths compared after resolving /tmp to /private/tmp, since git reports real paths), and offers Create branch “name” from what's typed, spaces made dashes. A switch, create, previous or pull gives every thread in that folder the new branch, and opening ⌘K asks for the branch again, since a terminal can move it. Checked on the Debug build in the alpha test repository: 'try me' offered Create branch “try-me”, which put the repository and the title capsule on try-me; Switch branch › main went back; with try-me given a different greet.swift and main's greet.swift uncommitted, try-me showed 'Uncommitted changes to greet.swift would be overwritten. Commit or stash them first.' in the palette; oricode/t-2ffdd2 read 'Open in its own thread' and opened it; there, Switch, Create and Previous branch were faint with 'This thread's branch is its own'. The engine suite passes, 33 tests. try-me was deleted afterwards. Pull, Push and Open on GitHub were run only in the engine tests, not from the app.

#### K-112 · A terminal over the glass
⌘J (View › Terminal) slides a terminal down from under the title bar the way Changes arrives. It stops 12pt above the composer, so the composer stays in view. ⌘J is used because backtick can't be typed on Meriç's Turkish layout. App/Terminal/TerminalSession.swift runs one login zsh per working folder, so a worktree thread gets its own. It starts on first use in the folder, never in a folder that has gone, with an environment cleaned the way the engine cleans the CLI's, and TERM_PROGRAM=OriCode. The shell stays alive while the terminal is hidden. The text is SF Mono on a clear background, with a muted palette, no accent-coloured selection, an overlay scroller and optionAsMetaKey off, so Option still types @ and {. Rule 2 keeps its meaning: at an idle prompt Esc hides the terminal. While a program holds the foreground (vim, fzf, claude), Esc goes to that program, and ⌘J or a click on the transcript puts the terminal away. The brief says so in one line. While the terminal has focus, the image-paste monitor and the ask's Return leave it alone. runInTerminal(_:in:) types a command into an idle shell and refuses a busy one, saying why.
Done when: ⌘J in a project slides in a shell, and `echo $TERM_PROGRAM $PWD` prints OriCode and the folder. Option-Q types @, Tab completes, and ⌘C and ⌘V work. Esc in vim leaves insert mode with the terminal still shown, and Esc at an idle prompt hides it. ⌘J brings it back with its scrollback. A worktree thread gets its own shell, `exit` closes the terminal, and Return typed while an ask waits doesn't allow it. Needs Meriç's eye on the Esc behaviour and on how the terminal sits over the glass.
Notes: App/Terminal holds it. TerminalSession runs a login shell for a folder in SwiftTerm's LocalProcessTerminalView, through a subclass that won't let a drag in it move the window; the store keeps one per folder, and one whose shell has exited is replaced on the next ⌘J. The text is the first fixed-pitch Nerd Font with letters in it when one is installed, and SF Mono otherwise: Meriç's Powerlevel10k prompt drew its icons as boxes in SF Mono, and the system font ignores a cascade list, so here it's MesloLGS NF, the font that prompt was made for. The background is clear, the sixteen colours are muted, the selection is white, and this version's scroller is hidden rather than overlaid. The shell's environment is an allowlist of what an app opened from the Dock is given, plus TERM, COLORTERM, TERM_PROGRAM=OriCode and its version, and LANG from the Mac's language and region when there's none, so nothing from a terminal or a Claude Code session that opened the app (CLAUDECODE, GIT_EDITOR=true, TMUX) gets in; Meriç's ~/.spawnrc sets LANG and LC_ALL to C.UTF-8 in every interactive shell anyway. SwiftTerm 1.11.2 forks without closing descriptors, which left every shell holding the engine's pipes and engine.log, so the app's descriptors are marked close-on-exec before each shell starts, and the engine's ends of its pipes as it starts. It also stops watching the shell once the pty reports its end, which comes first, so it never reported an exit or reaped the shell; the session watches the pid itself. Ending a shell sends SIGHUP, since terminate()'s SIGTERM is ignored by an interactive zsh and leaves the pty open. Busy means another process group has the terminal, or the shell's own group has other members, which is how zsh runs fzf's key bindings. The overlay's host keeps the terminal's size until SwiftUI gives it a real one: a zero-width first layout rewrapped the scrollback to one column for good. The terminal sits under the other panels, so ⌘K, ⌘P, Changes and the file viewer open over it and Esc gives it the keyboard back as they go; the model picker, which rises into its place, sends it away. Esc's stack checks the drawer's rename field before the terminal, and the terminal's step lets Esc through while it's busy; the composer leaves the keyboard alone while the terminal is up. A review in three lenses, each finding handed to a second reader to refute, found fourteen problems, among them the descriptors, the environment, the unreaped exit and the panels opening underneath; all are fixed but one, that the ^U runInTerminal types first clears only the current line of a continuation prompt. Checked on the Debug build: ⌘J slid in a shell and `echo $TERM_PROGRAM $PWD` printed OriCode and the thread's folder, Option-Q typed @, Tab completed ech to echo, Esc during `sleep` and in vim's insert mode went to the program with the terminal still up, Esc at the prompt hid it and ⌘J brought back its scrollback, `exit` closed it with the shell reaped and the next ⌘J started a new one, ⌘K opened over it and Esc gave the shell back the keys, the model picker sent it away, lsof showed the shell holding nothing of the app's, and make test passes. Not checked: ⌘C and ⌘V, which are SwiftTerm's copy: and paste: behind the system Edit menu, since checking them means touching Meriç's clipboard; a drag to select, which the agent's drag didn't reach; a worktree thread's own shell, which follows from one shell per folder; and Return with an ask waiting, since the card stops listening while the terminal shows. Needs Meriç's eye on the Esc behaviour and on the terminal over the glass.
Commit: a88dd4d

#### K-113 · The terminal at quit
Quitting with a command still running asks first: "A command is still running in the terminal", with Quit and Cancel. Quitting ends every shell, deleting a worktree thread ends its folder's shell, and while any shell exists the app holds a user-initiated activity so macOS doesn't throttle it.
Done when: quitting with `sleep 100` running asks first, Cancel keeps it running, and after quitting `ps` shows no leftover -zsh from OriCode.
Notes: Quitting asks through an NSAlert from the app delegate's applicationShouldTerminate while any shell is busy, naming what runs where: "Quitting OriCode stops sleep in alpha." The name is the foreground group's, or the first other member of the shell's own group, so fzf counts. applicationWillTerminate hangs every shell up. Ending a shell is a SIGHUP, as closing a terminal window is, and zsh passes it on to its jobs; the session stays in the store until its watcher has reaped the shell, then goes a second later, once the terminal has slid away, so a folder that's gone doesn't keep a dead shell's pty open. Deleting a worktree thread ends its folder's shell unless another thread works there, and removing a project ends the shells of its folder and its worktrees. The store holds a user-initiated activity while any shell is alive and lets it go with the last one. A shell that fails to start (no pty left, no fork) isn't kept, so the next ⌘J tries again, and runInTerminal says the shell couldn't start rather than that the folder is gone. The engine's six pipe descriptors are now marked close-on-exec before it starts, not after, which closes the last window in which a shell forked at the same moment could take them. Checked on the Debug build: with `sleep 100` running, Quit asked "A command is still running in the terminal" with Quit and Cancel; Cancel kept the app and the sleep; Quit ended the app, and ps showed neither OriCode's -zsh, the sleep nor the engine afterwards. New Thread on Its Own Branch made .worktrees/t-e8409d, ⌘J started a shell there (lsof showed its cwd), and deleting the thread with its worktree hung that shell up and reaped it while the terminal started one in the project's folder for the thread that took its place. make test passes. Not checked: App Nap's own view of the activity, which no tool here reads.

#### K-114 · The terminal in ⌘K
The command center reaches the terminal through runInTerminal and nothing else. Terminal (⌘J) shows or hides it. Run in terminal… asks for a command inline, with the last ten as its rows, and types it into the folder's shell. Continue in Claude Code releases the thread's CLI and types `claude --resume <session>` into the terminal. It is unavailable while that folder's terminal is busy, and its line warns that turns taken there won't show in the app.
Done when: ⌘K › Run in terminal… › `git status` shows its output in the terminal, a busy shell refuses it with a note, and Continue in Claude Code opens that session in the terminal.
Notes: App/Terminal/PaletteTerminal.swift adds the terminal's rows after git's. Terminal (⌘J) says Hide terminal while it shows. Run in terminal… is a level whose rows are the last ten commands run from it, newest first, kept in the defaults key terminalRecents, with a Run “…” row for whatever's typed. Continue in Claude Code closes the thread's CLI in the engine, then types `cd '<folder>' && claude --resume '<session>'` with both values in single quotes; it's faint with the reason when there's no thread, no session yet, a turn running or the terminal busy, and its line says turns taken there won't show here. All three go through runInTerminal. Typing now waits for a new shell's first output, because a line typed sooner was echoed once above the prompt, and a shell that has asked for bracketed paste gets the line as a paste. Checked on the Debug build: 'terminal' found the three rows; Run in terminal… › git status opened the terminal and ran it, cleanly once the wait was in; the next ⌘K had Run in terminal… under Recent and git status inside it; with `sleep 30` running, that row left the shell alone and said 'The terminal is busy; the command wasn't typed.', and Continue in Claude Code was faint with 'The terminal is busy'; after the sleep it typed the cd and claude --resume with the thread's session, and Claude Code opened on its folder-trust question, where No, exit left without trusting the scratch folder. Nothing was sent to Claude.
Commit: 8345e55

#### K-115 · Your own actions
Meriç can add their own rows to ⌘K. Each action has a name and a command, runs either in the terminal or quietly through a new engine method shell.run, can ask for {input}, can ask before running, and can be kept to one project. The placeholders are {cwd}, {project}, {projectName}, {branch}, {thread}, {session} and {input}. Each value is single-quoted before it reaches the shell, and an action whose placeholder has no value (no branch, no session yet) is unavailable, with the reason. The actions live in ~/Library/Application Support/OriCode/actions.json, never in a repository file. They are edited in a new Settings › Actions pane made of native rows, Toggles, Menus and a Form sheet. The first run seeds five examples: Branch from origin/main, Stash changes, Pop stash, Open pull request and Run tests. Quiet actions report their last line through the note under the composer. Custom actions get no key shortcuts until changeable shortcuts come off REA.
Done when: Branch from origin/main asks for a name and makes that branch in the terminal. A quiet Stash changes reports its line. An input of `a'b $(x)` reaches the shell as literal text, and Tests/CustomActionTests.swift and engine/test/shell.test.ts pass.
Notes: App/Actions holds it. A CustomAction is a name, a command, where it runs (the terminal, or quietly through the engine), whether it asks first, and an optional project; an entry written by hand needs only the name and the command. CustomActionStore keeps them in Application Support/OriCode/actions.json. The first run writes the five examples. A file that doesn't read is left untouched, and Settings says so. The file is read again whenever Settings or ⌘K opens and before any change, so an edit made by hand isn't saved over, and an id repeated by a copied entry gets one of its own. ActionLine fills the placeholders in one pass that reads the command's quoting the way a shell does. A bare placeholder becomes its value in single quotes, and one inside "…" or '…' closes that quote around its own, so `git commit -m "{input}"` stays safe. One inside $(…) or backticks can't be quoted, so the action is unavailable with that reason and the form won't save it. A missing value (no branch, no session yet) also makes an action unavailable, with the reason. {input} is asked for in ⌘K, with the whole line shown under the field as it's typed, and an action that asks first shows its line as a Run row. Quiet actions go through the engine's new shell.run: the user's shell -c in the folder, stdin closed, in a process group of its own, with SIGTERM at two minutes and SIGKILL two seconds after, and every one still running stops when the engine exits. The last line of output, without colour codes, becomes the note under the composer, and a failure stays in ⌘K. Settings › Actions lists the actions in a card of native rows, each with a menu (Edit…, Duplicate, Move Up, Move Down, Delete), plus Add Action… and Show in Finder, and edits one in a grouped Form sheet. A review of K-113 to K-115 in three lenses, each finding handed to a second reader to refute, found fifteen problems, all fixed here. Run in terminal's Return ran the top fuzzy match among the recents, so `git push` could run a recent `git push --force-with-lease`; the typed line now comes first, and only an exact recent replaces it. A placeholder inside the command's own quotes undid the quoting, which the pass above fixes. A control character in a value could end the bracketed paste, erase a quote, or with a tab typed before the shell takes pastes start a completion that rewrites the quoting, so runInTerminal refuses a line with any control character but a newline. The composer could send into a session handed to the terminal's claude and fork it; it now refuses with a note, keeping the text, while that terminal is busy. Continue in Claude Code could close a CLI with background tasks out. The quit question missed background and stopped jobs, and now counts the shell's children. Deleting a worktree thread or removing a project now says what running command it stops. A quiet action that ignored SIGTERM hung its request and outlived the engine. Actions kept to a removed project now go back to every project. Checked on the Debug build: the first launch wrote the five examples; Stash changes and Pop stash ran quietly on the scratch repo with their last lines as notes; with the input a'b $(x), Branch from origin/main's field showed `git switch -c 'a'\''b $(x)' origin/main`; Settings › Actions listed the actions, and Add Action… saved `echo {projectName} from {thread}` asking first, whose Run row ran `echo 'alpha' from 'Reply with the word draft.'` in the terminal; typing git st in Run put Run “git st” above the recent git status; the composer refused to send while Continue in Claude Code's claude held the session; the quit question named a `sleep 300 &` job; deleting a worktree thread said sleep ran there and stopped it. CustomActionTests (eight, running expanded lines through zsh and bash) and shell.test.ts (eight) pass with the rest of make test. Not checked: Return in an input level, since the agent's Return types a newline into a field, and the row menus, which the agent can't open. The test action was taken back out of actions.json by hand.
Commit: bf5dc4e

#### K-118 · SwiftTerm 1.20
Meriç asked for the Metal Toolchain and the newest SwiftTerm. `xcodebuild -downloadComponent MetalToolchain` installed it (839 MB, build 27A266a), and project.yml moves SwiftTerm from 1.11.2 to 1.20.0, whose Metal shader needs the toolchain to compile even though OriCode keeps SwiftTerm's default CoreText drawing. 1.20 generates build info through a package plugin, so every xcodebuild line in the Makefile passes -skipPackagePluginValidation, which command-line builds need for any package plugin. Two of the terminal's workarounds go, because 1.20 fixes what they covered: the layer background set by hand (it keeps that in step with the clear background) and the session's own exit watcher (it no longer drops the exit when the pty's end comes first). The rest stay, since 1.20 still needs them: the scroller hidden by hand (it is overlay-style now, but with no scroll view around it nothing fades it, so it draws a dark strip), descriptors marked close-on-exec (it still forks without closing any), SIGHUP to end a shell (its terminate still sends SIGTERM, which zsh ignores), the host that keeps the terminal's size, and the view that won't drag the window. THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES says 1.20.0.
Done when: `make run`, `make test` and `make app` build with SwiftTerm 1.20.0, and on the Debug build the terminal still does what K-112 to K-115 checked: `echo $TERM_PROGRAM $PWD`, the Powerlevel10k icons, Esc at a prompt and in vim, ⌘J's scrollback, `exit` reaped with the next ⌘J starting a fresh shell, the quit question, Run in terminal and a quiet action.
Notes: `xcodebuild -downloadComponent MetalToolchain` fetched 839 MB and installed Metal Toolchain 27A266a. project.yml pins SwiftTerm 1.20.0, which resolved and built, with its shader compiled into SwiftTerm_SwiftTerm.bundle as default.metallib. OriCode keeps SwiftTerm's default CoreText drawing, since the Metal renderer is opt-in. The Makefile's three xcodebuild lines pass -skipPackagePluginValidation for 1.20's build-info plugin, and the README and CLAUDE.md now name the toolchain as a build requirement. Two workarounds went: the clear background no longer has to be set on the layer by hand, and the session hears its shell end through SwiftTerm's own processTerminated, which 1.20 no longer drops when the pty's end comes first, instead of through a watcher of its own. The scroller had to stay hidden after all. 1.20's is overlay-style, but it stands outside any scroll view, so nothing fades it, and the first build showed it as a dark strip down the terminal's right edge; hidden, its width goes to the text. The close-on-exec sweep, SIGHUP to end a shell, the host that keeps the terminal's size and the view that won't drag the window stay, since 1.20 still forks without closing descriptors, still ends shells with SIGTERM and still doesn't override mouseDownCanMoveWindow. None of the SwiftTerm API OriCode calls changed. Checked on the Debug build: `echo $TERM_PROGRAM $PWD` printed OriCode and the folder; the Powerlevel10k icons drew; Esc at a prompt hid the terminal, and ⌘J brought its scrollback back; Esc in vim's insert mode stayed with vim; `exit` closed the terminal with the shell reaped, and the next ⌘J started a fresh shell that lsof showed holding none of the app's descriptors; the quit question named sleep in alpha, and quitting left no shell, sleep or engine; Run in terminal ran git status; Stash changes and Pop stash ran quietly with their notes. make run, make test and make app pass, and the installed Release build, which carries the metallib, launched from a clean environment and opened its terminal.

#### K-119 · Every model's Default and Ultracode
Meriç found no Ultracode stop on Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5 or any other model, and after Back to Defaults the level read Default with nothing on the slider. The engine reads each model's default level and Ultracode by switching two idle CLIs through the models, and Claude Code confirms a model named by its full id (Fable 5.1, among the main ones) with a one-token request it waits five seconds for. Right after Claude Code updated, that wait ran out, withDefaults threw, and no model got its default or its Ultracode. Each switch now fails on its own with a bounded wait, the list always follows as the models event, and a model the probe couldn't read takes the settings level or the catalog's Default. Ultracode needs a model with xhigh and workflows on, so the Ultracode CLI is switched to aliases only, and its answer goes to every xhigh model; Opus 5, Opus 4.8 and Opus 4.7 under More models get it too, and Opus 4.6 and Sonnet 4.6, which have no xhigh, don't. Hello's rows start on the catalog's Default. Until the models event arrives, a thread's stored Ultracode is kept and sent, not dropped.
Done when: the picker shows the Ultracode stop on Default, Opus 5.5, Fable 5.1, Sonnet 5, Opus 5, Opus 4.8 and Opus 4.7, and none on Haiku, Opus 4.6 or Sonnet 4.6; Back to Defaults puts the thumb on the ringed Default stop at Medium; the engine's tests cover a refused, a stuck and a silent probe; and make test passes.
Notes: The cause: Claude Code confirms a model named by its full id (claude-fable-5-1[1m], among the main ones) with a one-token API request and gives set_model five seconds for it. At the 13:35 launch, three minutes after Claude Code updated to 2.1.281, that ran out, withDefaults threw, and learnDefaults sent no models event, so every model kept hello's empty default and no Ultracode. withDefaults now reads the plain CLI and the Ultracode CLI in parallel, with a bounded wait on each step: a refused switch costs only that model its reading, a switch that never answers ends that CLI's run with what it has, and the event always goes out. A model the probe didn't read gets settled's level: the settings' effortLevel when the model has it, else the catalog's Default, brought under any maxEffortLevel. Claude Code runs Ultracode on a model that keeps xhigh when workflows are on, so the Ultracode CLI is switched to aliases only, which send no request, and its answer goes to every xhigh model. Probed on 2.1.281, Opus 5, Opus 4.8 and Opus 4.7 run it, and Opus 4.6 turns xhigh into high. The event says whether Ultracode was learned; until it is, the app assumes Ultracode on xhigh models, so a thread's stored Ultracode shows and goes out as it is instead of being dropped. Hello's rows start where the user's settings put Default (read from settings.json in Claude Code's config folder) or on the catalog's Default, so the slider is never empty. A review, each finding handed to a second reader to refute, found that an earlier guard sent a stored Ultracode while the picker showed Default, and dropped it for good when the Ultracode CLI never answered; it also found that hello's catalog default (High for Sonnet 5) could turn a High pick into Default. The assumption and the settings read replace that guard. Not handled: 2.1.281 also keeps a per-model effortLevel under modelSettings, which settled doesn't read, so a model the probe misses can show the top-level level until its thread's CLI reports its own. Checked: the engine run alone in the app's environment gave every model a default in hello, then Ultracode on Default, Opus 5.5, Fable 5.1, Sonnet 5, Opus 5, Fable 5, Opus 4.8 and Opus 4.7, with Medium from the settings. On the Debug build, Opus 5 and Opus 5.5 showed the Ultracode stop; the arrows reached it on Opus 5.5 with its rays lit; Sonnet 5 kept it; Opus 4.6 dropped it to Default and showed four stops; and Back to Defaults put the thumb on the ringed Default stop at Medium. Fable 5.1 and Haiku were checked in the engine's list, not clicked. The engine's 53 tests cover a refused, a stuck and a silent probe, and make test passes. Meriç's picks were put back to Opus 5 at Max.

#### K-120 · Review
Meriç asked for a diff section behind a button at the top right, one people would pick OriCode for. Five readers went through what people say about diff views in agent tools, IDEs, git clients and pull-request review: Reddit through the Arctic Shift archive, since reddit.com refuses the tools, and Hacker News, GitHub issues and the Cursor forum. What came up most: edits that never reach the review because the agent made them through the shell; a pending Keep state that drifts from git; no way to take back one hunk, or to undo taking it back; files in alphabetical order with nothing to say which prompt made what; no way to see only what changed since you last looked; and notes pasted into the chat by hand. The Review answers each and takes the Changes sheet's place.

⌘⇧D, or a button at the right end of the title bar's row that shows the working tree's counts, brings it down over the transcript where the terminal comes, and closes the terminal. It reads the working tree against HEAD in the engine, tracked and untracked files alike, so a change a command made is there too. It tells the diff the way the thread went: each of your messages as the transcript shows it, then what its edits changed, file by file in the order the turn first touched them, with the builds and tests it ran; then, under "Not from this thread's edits", whatever no edit here made. A line in Claude's hunk that no edit made is marked. Lines are numbered on both sides, in the code's colours, with the words that changed lit. Space marks a hunk reviewed and moves on; a mark is kept by what the hunk changes, so it survives a relaunch, and a hunk Claude changes again comes back with the lines you saw dimmed. N writes a note for Claude, and the notes go as one message. ⌫ takes a hunk back out of the file, with Undo, ⌘Z and ⌘⇧Z. Commit takes everything, or only what's reviewed. It labels a block that moved, a test whose expectation changed or that was switched off, a hunk that changes only spacing, and a lockfile.
Done when: on a scratch repo, a turn's hunks sit under its message and a change made outside the thread's edits sits in the last chapter; a hunk marked reviewed is still reviewed after a relaunch and comes back when it changes; a hunk taken back leaves the file and ⌘Z puts it back; Commit with some hunks reviewed commits only those and leaves what was staged by hand; the engine's and the app's tests cover the diff, a stale patch, the reviewed commit, the chapters and the signals; make test passes. Needs Meriç's eye on the look.
Notes: The engine's review.ts reads the diff with name-status, numstat and the patch, each keyed by path, and parses hunks by their header counts, so a line of code that looks like a header can't end one. Untracked files are read as new ones, an empty one with no lines. Every path is from the repository's top: the Changes sheet committed paths git gives from the top while running in the thread's folder, which went wrong for a project in a subfolder of its repository. git.status went with the sheet, and git.message now takes the diff the app is about to commit. App/Review holds the rest. Provenance reads which turn wrote each changed line from the thread's own Edit, MultiEdit and Write calls, by file and text, and which builds and tests each turn ran from its Bash calls through a known runner (make test counts, cat test.txt doesn't). ReviewBook makes the chapters. A hunk belongs to the latest turn that wrote one of its lines that says something, since a brace or a blank line matches anywhere. A mark is the hunk's path, status and changed lines hashed, untracked and added counting as one, so it holds while edits above move the hunk or the file is staged; marks go once HEAD moves and their file has left the diff, or after thirty days, so a stash leaves them alone, and a hunk marked again drops only older marks of hunks no longer there. Taking back goes through git apply -R for a modified or renamed file's hunks, all of a patch or none, and out of the index too where they were staged; a new, deleted, binary, too-large or non-UTF-8 file is taken back whole: whatever sits at its paths goes to the Trash, and HEAD's version and index entry come back. A step writes down what it did as it goes, and undoing it undoes exactly that, HEAD's versions going to the Trash rather than away, and the index entries they replaced coming back. What the review does to the files runs one step at a time, so an Undo pressed mid-step waits instead of being lost. The undo action holds its step, because the undo manager keeps no reference to its target: the first build crashed on Redo reading a freed one. Commit Reviewed builds an index from HEAD in a temporary file, adds the whole files, applies the chosen hunks, and commits with the user's hooks and signing, refusing if another commit lands meanwhile; the real index then takes the new version of whole files and the committed hunks on top of whatever was staged by hand. The signals live in Signals.swift: a deleted run of three or more lines that says something, found at 80% or more in one added run elsewhere, is a move, with braces and blank lines going along when the other end has them; a hunk in a test file that deletes an assertion line or adds a skip is flagged; package managers' lockfiles fold. The chapters, words and moves are worked out off the main thread once per read, and a mark only lays the marks over them again; one read runs at a time, and edits arriving meanwhile make one more. Rows are a flat lazy list, a hunk past 400 lines shows its first 200 until asked, and colours arrive in batches from the highlighter's actor. The diff took 63 ms for this repository's 25 changed files and 3,358 lines. Five research reports fed the design. The loudest single thread was Cursor's forum topic on diffs missing after 2.5 (about 236 posts, nine people downgrading), then Codex's /undo request (460 👍), Claude Code's #33932 for a review set (198 👍) and zed#59157 for comments that go to the agent (103 👍). What people loved and nobody's agent view had: hunk-level review since you last looked, moved code shown as moved, and a flag when a test's assertion changes. A review of the change, each finding reproduced in scratch repositories or a harness built from a copy of App/Review, found fifteen problems, all fixed here. Four could lose work: the footer's Undo during Write Message cleared itself and put nothing back; a failed Redo left a stale record that the next Undo acted on, deleting a file outright; taking back a rename overwrote an untracked file at its old name; and a staged new file taken back stayed staged, so the next commit brought it back. A non-UTF-8 file had U+FFFD written over its bytes by its own hunks. git listing one path twice, deleted from the index and untracked on disk, crashed the app on every read. Colours cached by what a hunk changes showed another hunk's lines and could index past the end. Commit failed on a staged git rm or git mv; Commit Reviewed unstaged hand-staged hunks of the files it committed and refused a force-added file; a partly reviewed rename lost its marks without being committed. Two hunks adding the same line couldn't both stay reviewed, marks vanished with a stash, and git add made a reviewed new file unreviewed. A CRLF file's colours went on one row, a function line holding \r dropped its hunk, J and K walked into folded chapters, a note's editor could vanish and keep the keys, and each Space rebuilt everything on the main thread (1.8 s for 3,000 hunks in Release). Checked on the Debug build with the scratchpad's alpha repo, nothing sent to Claude. The button at the top right counted +5, then +12 −11 after a moved function, a changed test, a changed lockfile and a changed line were made by hand. A thread whose edit added "// Greets with a name." showed that hunk under its message, and everything else under "Not from this thread's edits"; a thread whose edit had since been replaced showed nothing under its own. Space folded a hunk and moved on, a turn with every hunk reviewed folded under its message, and the marks survived relaunches. A note appeared under its hunk and the footer offered to send it; it was discarded, not sent. A hunk of greet.swift was taken back and came back with the footer's Undo and with Edit › Undo; an untracked file went to the Trash and came back. Commit Reviewed made a commit of the two reviewed files and left the two unreviewed ones untracked. The moved function showed "moved to Display.swift:2, changed on the way" and "moved from User.swift:6, changed on the way", dimmed except the line whose default changed and the new comment; the test hunk showed "changes what a test expects" with "hi" and "hello" lit; the lockfile sat folded. O opened the file over the review and Esc closed only the file. After the review's fixes, a staged new file taken back left the disk and the index, and the footer's Undo brought it back staged; a staged git rm taken back put the file back in both, and Undo staged its removal again. While checking, the file viewer turned out to slide under a pinned drawer and hide the start of every line; it now sits beside it. The engine's review.test.ts (fifteen tests) and ReviewTests (twenty-three) pass with the rest of make test: 68 engine tests and 48 Swift tests. Not handled: a side-by-side view; more lines of context around a hunk; a search within the review; another thread's edits in the same folder land under "Not from this thread's edits" rather than under that thread; a renamed or new file commits whole; Write Message wasn't clicked, since it's a Haiku call; Edit › Undo worked with the window in front. Edit › Redo crashed the first build; after the fix it wasn't pressed again, since the agent couldn't bring the window forward, and the fix was checked in a script that shows the undo manager letting go of its target.

#### K-121 · 0.1.0-rc
Meriç, once the review was in: the app becomes 0.1.0-rc, the candidate for the first public release, and from here only fixes are taken. The version reads 0.1.0-rc everywhere it's kept: project.yml's MARKETING_VERSION, engine/version.ts, and the engine's package.json and lock file. The board says so where it says how to work and how versions go: a card is taken only if it fixes something broken in what's there, and anything new waits under a heading of its own until 0.1.0. Meriç won't pay for a Developer ID, so OriCode is never notarized, and a copy downloaded from GitHub is stopped the first time it opens; the README says how to let it through, in System Settings › Privacy & Security › Open Anyway. Meriç makes it once in Keychain Access (Certificate Assistant › Create a Certificate, Code Signing, named OriCode) and keeps a backup, and make app signs with it instead of ad hoc, falling back to ad hoc on a Mac without it. macOS then keeps a grant like Screen Recording from one build to the next, and every release carries the same designated requirement, which the updater (K-135) relies on. It lives in Meriç's login keychain with codesign allowed to use its key, so signing asks nothing, and its private key is nowhere else: a backup is Keychain Access › File › Export Items, or Time Machine's copy of the keychain. The Makefile's SIGN finds it with security find-identity and falls back to ad hoc, and make app signs with it. The README gains Opening it the first time. #### K-135 · OriCode updates itself
Anyone who installs 0.1.0 only hears about 0.2.0 if the app tells them. Sparkle, the usual updater for Mac apps outside the App Store, reads the appcast on main once a day, downloads the release it names, checks its EdDSA signature against the key in the app, installs it and relaunches. Its private key stays in Meriç's Keychain, and the app is built without the hardened runtime, so Sparkle loads under K-134's certificate with no Developer ID. OriCode draws its own UI for it, as Meriç asked, instead of Sparkle's windows: once there's an update, a circle hangs off the title capsule's right end, however wide the capsule is, with an update arrow in it and the notes on hover. A click downloads it, the circle filling from the bottom as it goes, and it turns into a restart arrow; a second click restarts into the new version. OriCode Molten has no feed and never updates itself. Releases go out through a new make release: it builds and signs as make app does, zips the app, signs the zip with the EdDSA key, publishes the GitHub release with gh and points appcast.xml at it. Publishing is Meriç's, so Meriç runs it in Terminal, as with make app. Taken during the freeze at Meriç's word. On Linear as REA-172.
Done when: an OriCode numbered as 0.1.0-rc finds a test release one version ahead in an appcast, shows the circle beside the capsule with the notes on hover, fills it while downloading, and after the second click comes back as that version with its threads and settings intact.
Notes: Sparkle 2.10.0 comes in through SwiftPM. Updates.swift is its user driver: an SPUUpdater with OriCode as the SPUUserDriver, keeping a phase (idle, available, downloading with its fraction, ready) and holding Sparkle's reply until the circle is clicked. OriCode › Check for Updates… asks at once and says "OriCode X is the newest." under the composer when there's nothing. UpdateCircle.swift draws it as an overlay on the capsule's trailing edge, 6pt out, so it follows the pill at any width; the first cut sat at the end of the capsule's 520pt frame, far from a short pill, which Meriç caught. The Info.plist takes its versions from the build settings, the feed from ORICODE_FEED_URL, empty in Debug, and the public EdDSA key; generate_keys put the private half in the login keychain. make app numbers builds with git rev-list --count, make release runs scripts/release.sh, and appcast.xml starts as an empty channel, so a check before the first release finds nothing rather than a 404. Checked with the Release build copied into a test app with its own bundle id, numbered 1000 as 0.1.0-rc, beside a copy numbered 1001 as 0.1.1-test, zipped, signed with sign_update and offered by a local appcast over http://127.0.0.1, since Sparkle refuses file:// feeds. At launch it fetched the appcast and the circle appeared beside the capsule with its arrow, and renders of the face at 45%, 80% and ready show the fill with the arrow dark under it. Meriç didn't get to the two clicks, so the install was checked through Sparkle's silent path, with SUAutomaticallyUpdate on for the test app only: it downloaded the zip and verified it, and on quit Autoupdate replaced the bundle within a second with build 1001, still signed by the OriCode certificate. Relaunched, it ran as 0.1.1-test with its project, store and a planted setting intact. Meriç: on a question with options, it isn't obvious which one is picked. With two questions or more a click on an option picks it without answering, and the picked button looked like the others, since `.tint` does nothing to a bordered button on macOS; a checked box was a small grey check and nothing more. Options become rows in the brief's own tints: nothing behind a row at rest, white at 7% under the pointer, white at 10% behind a picked one, and a checkmark at the end of the row when the question takes one answer. A question that takes several keeps its native checkboxes, and each checked row lights the same way. The rows reach 10pt past the column so their words line up with the question's. What's typed in Other is the answer, so typing there puts out that question's picks, and picking an option empties Other.
Done when: renders of the real card over a dark and a bright backdrop show a picked option lit with its checkmark among unlit ones and the rows of checked boxes lit; the Debug build builds and make test passes. Needs Meriç's eye on a real question.
Notes: QuestionForm draws each option as an OptionRow. A one-answer option stays a Button, as K-09 made it, so a click on a lone question's option still answers it, and its row lights with the checkmark at its end and tells VoiceOver it's selected; a several-answer option stays the native checkbox Toggle, inside a row that lights while it's checked. Both fade in and out with the brief's fade. Checked: the real QuestionForm and OptionRow, compiled offscreen with stubs of AppModel and PendingAsk, rendered a card with one question of each kind: before the change, a picked Plain JSON looked exactly like SQLite and In memory; after it, Plain JSON's row is lit with its checkmark and the checked Type-check and Lint rows are lit, over a dark and a bright backdrop at 560pt, and at 400pt. Another thread had uncommitted work in this folder and was testing with OriCode Molten, so this change was built on its own in a scratch worktree of HEAD whose Debug app was renamed to keep its test host out of Molten's store: it built, and make test's two halves passed, 68 engine tests and 53 Swift tests. Not checked: hover, clicks, typing in Other, or a real question in the app, since the thread this was built in can't see or press the window.
Commit: c16a909

#### K-138 · Pick up after a quit
A stray ⌘Q in the middle of a turn stopped the thread where it was, and nothing picked it up until you wrote to it. OriCode now does what the Claude Code app does. A thread still working when OriCode quits, on a turn or with a subagent or background command out, is marked, and the next launch sends "The app was quit while you were working. Please continue from where you left off." into its session as a message of its own, on screen or not, so the transcript says why the thread moved. A thread that was waiting on you for an answer or a permission comes back still waiting, with the call it holds. Answering the card resumes the session with the answer, a permission given covers the same call when Claude makes it again, and Stop ends the turn there. Stop (⌘.) is still how a turn ends; a quit isn't one. A SIGTERM, which is how make run and make app quit OriCode, counts as a quit. On Linear as REA-150.
Done when: with a thread running, ⌘Q and reopening OriCode shows that line in the thread and Claude goes on with the work without anything typed, while a thread that was waiting on a question when the app quit comes back still waiting.
Notes: Chat.quitMidTurn is set as the app terminates, for each conversation with a turn running or tasks out, and saved with what had streamed. Once the engine is ready at launch, AppModel.pickUpAfterQuit sends the line into each marked thread that isn't waiting on you; one quit before its session began gets a note instead, since there's nothing to resume. A marked thread's last turn keeps the asks that were waiting (Conversation.askedBeforeQuit), running, with the calls they hold still open. Answering one records the answer, puts out the others and sends a message saying what was asked and what the user chose (AppModel.afterQuit); an allowed permission rides the send as `grant`, which the engine spends on the same call once, description aside, so it isn't asked twice. Stop records the asks cancelled and a stopped footer, with no time since the turn has none of its own. A session whose last CLI died with a command still running reports that command stopped when resumed, and the CLI answers its own report with a result, num_turns 0, before it takes up the message; the engine skips that result, which showed as an empty turn and could post Finished. The delegate turns SIGTERM into NSApp.terminate without the terminal question, and make run and make app wait up to five seconds for the app to go before opening or replacing it. Checked: three Haiku sessions from the SDK, cut off mid-command, mid-permission and mid-question, each resumed cleanly with the line or the answer, Claude making the same call again. In OriCode Molten, two threads seeded into its store on sessions cut off that way: at launch the working one, which wasn't the open one, got the line, re-ran `sleep 25` and said DONE, while the question thread sent nothing and kept its ask through three launches. A SIGTERM with a picked-up thread's command running in the background marked it, and the next launch sent the line again; Haiku then chose not to start a fourth `sleep` and said why. Fed a send with a grant from Terminal, on a session cut off while `echo hello > out.txt` waited for permission, the engine allowed Claude's repeated call without an ask and the file was written. QuitTests covers the replay, answering, Stop, marking and the messages, and grant.test.ts covers the matching. make test passes, 71 engine tests and 60 Swift tests. Not checked: ⌘Q itself, which goes through the same terminate path as the SIGTERM, and the clicks on a card from before a quit, since this thread can't press the window. Needs Meriç to ⌘Q a working thread once.

#### K-128 · Surfaces out of the capsule
Meriç's idea, parked by the freeze. The title capsule becomes where the command center, the file finder and the review come from, the way the Dynamic Island grows into whatever it's showing. ⌘K, ⌘P or ⌘⇧D stretches the capsule down into that surface, its top staying where the capsule's is, and Esc or a click elsewhere folds it back up into the capsule; the review's button at the top right opens it out of the capsule too, which was Meriç's call. The capsule holds one surface at a time, so opening one folds away the one that's up, and a row in the command center that opens the review turns one into the other. The capsule already moves over when the drawer is pinned, so each surface grows from wherever the capsule is, and pinning or unpinning the drawer with a surface open carries the two across together. With no project there's no capsule, and ⌘K opens the command center as it does now. The morph is SwiftUI's own, matchedGeometryEffect, since K-116 hasn't put the surfaces on glass. The terminal was this card's third surface until REA-152 took it into the conversation instead, so it keeps coming down from the top until that's built. On Linear as REA-151.
Done when: frames show the command center, the file finder and the review grow out of the capsule and fold back into it with the drawer pinned and unpinned, pinning the drawer with one open moves both, Esc and a click elsewhere still dismiss, and with Reduce Motion on they fade in and out instead.
Notes: App/Island.swift holds the capsule and its surfaces in one overlay, in the capsule's lane: 130pt in from both sides of the window, and 20pt from a pinned drawer. One piece of glass follows whichever of them is up through matchedGeometryEffect, the capsule's pill and each surface being the sources and the glass the follower, so the morph is a frame the move spring carries rather than a shape drawn frame by frame. A surface is seen only through a mask that follows the glass the same way, so nothing spills past the glass while it grows or folds; its contents come in 80ms after it starts and go in 100ms. The glass's corners are 14pt, which a 24pt capsule draws as round ends, and a surface's material fades in with it. The update circle stays outside the mask, on the capsule's end. The surfaces lose their own glass and fixed widths: the command center and the finder are 560pt at most and the review 960pt, all within the lane, so at 1180pt the review is 920pt wide where it was 960, and it starts 50pt higher. openInIsland keeps one surface up, so ⌘K over the review folds the review away where it used to open over it. With Reduce Motion there's no follower and no mask: each surface has its own glass and cross-fades with the capsule in place. Checked with frames of the real RootView and AppModel, on an in-memory store with a thread and a review in it, hosted in a window by a test run only locally and captured with cacheDisplay at about 8 frames a second: ⌘K, ⌘P and the review each grow from the capsule's pill with their top on its line and fold back into it on Esc; before the mask the command center's rows showed below the growing glass, and after it none did; with the drawer pinned the command center grows right of the drawer, unpinning with the review open slides the two across together, and a command center turning into the review shrinks and regrows its glass on the way; with Reduce Motion forced on for one run, the command center and the capsule cross-fade in place, where the first try clipped the command center to a growing rectangle because the mask stayed on. make test passes, 71 engine tests and 61 Swift tests. Not checked: a click elsewhere, which is the same tap as before, the capsule's hover, and the real window over Meriç's wallpaper. Needs Meriç's eye on the morph.
