#### K-140 · Files fold in the review
Meriç: a review with many files is one long run of lines, hard to read. A click on a file's header in the review closes it to that one line, its path, counts and a chevron, with a check once every hunk in it is reviewed, and another click opens it again. The first time the review opens in a folder, one file is open, the first with something to review, and every other is closed; files that arrive later come in closed. The keyboard reads one file at a time: ↑↓ and J K going into a closed file open it and close the one they left, and so does Space when it moves on to the next hunk to review in another file. A file closed under the keyboard is passed over whole, and Space, ⌫, N and O act only on a hunk you can see. The brief's review sentence says so.
Done when: a unit test covers the first look opening one file, the keyboard opening the file it goes into and closing the one it leaves, and passing over a file closed under it; renders of the real review show the first file open and the rest closed; make test passes. Needs Meriç's eye on a real review.
Notes: ReviewState keeps openFiles, the files whose hunks have rows, and the keyboard's logic moved onto it so a test can drive it. placeFiles opens the file of the first hunk to review, or of the first hunk, once per folder: when the panel appears, or when a read lands while it's up. move steps over every hunk of the chapters that aren't folded and skips the rest of a file closed under the keyboard, and select opens the file it lands in and closes the one it left; Space moves on through select too, and a take-back, a put-back or a hunk that's gone opens the file of the one selected in its place. toggle is the header's click, and closing a file lets go of a note being written in it. selectedUnit is the keyboard's hunk only while its file is open, and it's what Space, ⌫, N and O act on. A hunk knows its file section's id, which the book now builds its sections from. The header is one plain Button holding the chevron, which brightens under the pointer, the status, path and counts, and a check once every hunk is reviewed; Open stays a sibling button. Checked: theKeyboardReadsOneFileAtATime covers the first look with and without the top file reviewed, going into a closed file, and passing over one closed under the keyboard. Frames of the real RootView with a review of three files, shot in the test host of a scratch worktree: the review opened with TitleCapsule.swift's two hunks under a turned chevron and Island.swift and ReviewView.swift folded to their headers; three steps down, TitleCapsule.swift had folded and Island.swift was open with its hunk selected; a click on Island.swift's header folded it under the keyboard, and the next step passed it and opened ReviewView.swift. HEAD with this change, its Debug app renamed to keep clear of OriCode Molten, built and passed make test's two halves, 71 engine tests and 61 Swift tests. Not checked: real clicks and keys, or a long review, since the thread this was built in can't see or press the window.

#### K-141 · Go on when the session limit resets
Meriç, after this card's own thread met the session limit: the Claude Code app picks a thread up again once the five-hour limit resets, while OriCode said "Rate limited. Try again in a moment." and stopped for good. The engine now reads Claude's plan limits from the CLI's rate_limit_event messages, and a turn a limit refused ends with a `limited` event, which says which limit and when it resets, where that line was. The thread says "Stopped at Claude's session limit. This thread goes on when it resets at 23:30.", with Cancel, and once it resets, the thread is sent "The session limit has reset. Please continue from where you left off." as a message of its own, on screen or not, the way K-138 picks one up after a quit. A message sent before then, or Cancel, calls it off, and a thread away from the window says in its notification when it goes on. A weekly limit only says when it resets, since that can be days away.
Done when: the engine, fed what the CLI sent when this thread met the limit, answers with `limited` and the reset rather than an error; a unit test has a thread whose reset has passed sent the line through AppModel's own wait; renders of the real transcript show the line waiting with Cancel, after Cancel, and for a weekly limit; make test passes. Needs a real session limit to be seen end to end.
Notes: The CLI marks the refusal with a synthetic assistant message, error rate_limit, text "You've hit your session limit · resets 11:30pm (Europe/Istanbul)", after a rate_limit_event whose rate_limit_info is rejected, with resetsAt in seconds and rateLimitType five_hour. The engine keeps the latest rate_limit_info, holds the refusal until the turn's result, then sends `limited` when a rejected limit says when it resets and the old line when none does. Chat.resumeAt is set for the session limit, or for a limit that doesn't name itself and resets within six hours, and the line is stored as a `limited` event, so a relaunch still shows it and still waits. AppModel keeps one wait, for the soonest reset plus 20 seconds, on the continuous clock so a Mac asleep through the reset goes on when it wakes; when it's over, every thread due and not already running is sent the line, and a reset that passed while OriCode was closed is picked up once the engine is ready. A thread refused again after its reset, which a Mac's clock running ahead of Claude's would do, waits five more minutes rather than trying again at once. Checked: limit.test.ts feeds the engine that rate_limit_event, assistant message and result and gets `limited` with 1790281800000 and five_hour and no error, and gets the old line without the limit report; LimitTests covers the session limit waiting and surviving a replay, a weekly one not waiting, a refusal past the reset, Cancel and a message calling it off, and AppModel sending a thread whose reset had passed the line within its first wait; renders of the real transcript show "Stopped at Claude's session limit. This thread goes on when it resets at Fri 02:06." with Cancel, the same line after Cancel ending "which resets at Fri 02:06.", and the weekly one. make test passes, 75 engine tests and 67 Swift tests. Not checked: a real session limit reached and reset in the app, and the notification's words.

#### K-144 · Commands as blocks in the thread
The first of three cards for REA-152, the terminal in the conversation. A `!` at the start of the composer turns it into a shell prompt for the thread's folder, with a `$` before the text, until Esc or ⌫ in an empty prompt, and Return runs the command and keeps the prompt. The command runs in a terminal of its own, the user's login shell running it interactively so their PATH and aliases hold, and its block lands in the transcript between the messages: the command, then what it printed in the terminal's colours, following the output while it runs, with Stop, which is ⌃C. The block stays with the thread and shows again after a relaunch. Claude reads every block since your last message with the next one, the way Claude Code gives it its own `!` commands, as `<bash-input>` with the command and `<bash-stdout>` with what it printed since Claude last read it, so an error you hit reaches Claude without pasting it. A command left running keeps running while the thread goes on, and quitting asks first, as for the terminal. The ⌘J terminal stays as it is until K-146 takes its place.
Done when: in a thread, `!make test` runs in the thread's folder and its output shows as a block in the transcript, and Claude's next reply can quote a line of it.
Notes: App/Shell holds it. ShellBlock runs `$SHELL -l -i -c <command>` through SwiftTerm's LocalProcess in a 96-column, 24-row pty, with PAGER and GIT_PAGER set to cat so nothing waits on a pager nobody can page, and feeds a headless SwiftTerm Terminal that nothing draws; ShellRender reads that terminal back, every line from the top of the scrollback with the lines it wrapped joined, as an AttributedString in the old terminal's sixteen muted colours plus the 256 and true colours, and as plain text for Claude. The block redraws at most every 80ms and shows its last 400 lines. SwiftTerm reports waitpid's raw status, so the exit code is read out of it the way a shell says it, 128 and the signal for a signal. The thread keeps each block as a `shell` event with the command, the last 256KB of output and how many characters of its text Claude has read; the event is written when the command ends, and at quit, before the command is hung up with the app, and a block from an earlier launch is drawn once from its bytes off the main thread. A block's model is registered before its item goes into the thread: the first build did it the other way round, and a second command's block, whose view looked for it as it first drew, never learned it was running. Checked: ShellTests covers a replayed terminal's lines with a wrapped one joined, colours as runs, what Claude reads (a failed and a running command, and a long one cut to its last 30,000 characters), a real command's folder, output and exit code, Stop ending `sleep 30` with 130, and AppModel running a command in a thread, starting it and naming it, giving Claude the block once and storing it; frames of the real window show the shell prompt, `make test` running with Stop and then with FAIL in red and exit 2, and a second command live with its first line while the first has ended; and in that window, with the engine started, a Haiku thread asked which test had failed quoted the block's FAIL line and its expected 'b'. make test passes, 75 engine tests and 73 Swift tests. Not checked: the real composer's `!`, ⌫ and Esc under a finger, and a long-running server, which K-145 is about.

Each file's header gets a circle of its own, in the column of its hunks' circles, that marks every hunk in the file reviewed in one click, or unmarks them, and moves on the same way; it takes the place of the check. ← folds the file the keyboard is in and → opens it, as in a Finder list, and ⌥-click on a file's chevron folds or opens every file.

#### K-149 · The composer keeps the keyboard
Meriç, trying the shell prompt: Tab didn't complete anything, and typing then left the composer, so every next line needed a click in it; and what ⌫ was meant to do wasn't clear. Keys sent to the real window found the causes. Tab went unhandled and AppKit passed the keyboard on to the next view, a list elsewhere in the window. After Return at the prompt the rebuilt field was asked for the keyboard in the same update that made it, so the keyboard went to the window and the next keys to nothing. ⌘K and the review folding back into the capsule left it with nobody too. And ⌫ in an empty prompt did nothing, because the Backspace key sends DEL, 0x7F, while SwiftUI's .delete is 0x08. Now Tab never leaves the composer. At the prompt it completes the word being typed, the first word as a command from the user's own shell and any other as a path from the thread's folder, as far as the matches agree, with a folder's slash or a file's space. Several matches are listed above the composer, and Tab, ⇧Tab or the arrows go through them, while Esc puts the list away before anything else. In a message Tab completes a slash command, as before, or a path after `@`. The rebuilt field takes the keyboard once it's in the window, a surface folding back into the capsule hands the keyboard back to the composer, or to an open block, and ⌫ in an empty prompt turns it back into the composer. ↑ and ↓ bring back earlier commands at the prompt and earlier messages in an empty composer, as in a shell and in Claude Code. ⌘K's ⌫ back a level had the same DEL problem, and the effort rail's ⌫ back to Default now listens for the Delete command, the way the review's does; ⌘K's and ⌘P's Tab stay in their fields.
Done when: keys sent to the real window show Tab keeping the keyboard in the composer, `cat REA` and `hello @REA` completing, several matches listed and gone with Esc, the next command typed straight after Return, ⌫ in an empty prompt turning it back, ↑ and ↓ going through earlier commands, and the composer taking the keyboard back after ⌘K and the review; a unit test covers the completion; make test passes.
Notes: ShellCompletion is pure: the word is what follows the last space a backslash doesn't escape; the first word without a slash matches the shell's names; anything else is listed from its folder, the thread's folder unless it starts with / or ~, hidden entries only after a dot, and case-blind when nothing matches as typed; names are escaped as a shell reads them. The shell's names come from `$SHELL -l -i -c` once, zsh's commands, aliases, builtins, functions and reserved words, bash's compgen, without zsh's underscore functions, asked as the prompt opens; a Tab that comes first waits for them and lets go if you type on meanwhile. The composer keeps the list, which match it holds and the text Tab left, and typing anything else puts the list away; AppModel.composerMenu lets Esc reach it first. rebuild(after:) replaces send's rebuild and runs for commands too, 30ms after the new field. The island's fold calls returnKeyboard on the next turn of the run loop, which keeps away from a rename field, the picker and an open file. History at the prompt is the last 200 lines run from it, kept in the defaults; in a message it's the thread's own messages, less the lines OriCode sent for you. Checked with keys sent to the real window in a local harness and a log of who had the keyboard after each: before, Tab after "hello" gave it to a SwiftUIOutlineListView, Return at the prompt and closing ⌘K to the window, and closing the review to its host; after, the composer's field editor had it every time, with `cat README.md ` and `hello @README.md ` completed, `ls` listing ls, lsappinfo and lsof with the list shown above the composer, the first Esc putting it away and the second leaving the prompt, pwd then ls recalled with ↑ and gone with ↓, and ⌫ on an empty prompt turning it back. CompletionTests covers whole and partial matches, escaping, hidden files, commands and mentions. make test passes, 75 engine tests and 82 Swift tests, in a worktree of HEAD and this card, since the shared folder held another thread's workflow change halfway. #### K-143 · Workflows you can watch
Meriç: a thread can run a workflow, and OriCode shows nothing of it but the tool's line. The Workflow call becomes a card of its own in the thread, the way an edit's diff is: the workflow's name beside OriCode's mark, whose rays light for its agents at work and turn while it runs, how far it has got, and its phases in order, each with a bead per agent, faint while queued, lit while running, settled once done and red if it failed. A click opens the agents under their phases, each with the tool it's on or what it used, and a failure's reason. The phases the script names show from the start, and agents come in as the CLI reports them. The engine reads the workflow off the CLI's task messages, the progress snapshots it sends while one runs among them, and tells the app in a `workflow` event; the thread keeps the last one, and a workflow the engine lost shows as stopped. REA-161 can take the same readings into its list of what's running.
Done when: engine and Swift tests cover reading a snapshot, the script's phases and the grouping; frames of the real transcript show a running workflow and a finished one; make test passes. Needs a real workflow on Meriç's machine, since the snapshots were read in the CLI's code and haven't been seen on the wire.
Notes: engine/workflow.ts shapes the CLI's snapshot: the phases in order from its workflow_phase entries, and each workflow_agent as a label, phase, state (queued until it has started, then running, done or failed), tokens, tool calls, the tool it's on and what on, and an error; the log lines are left out. thread.ts takes a task_started whose task_type is local_workflow for a workflow, keeps its call and name, and sends `workflow` with the whole state when it starts, on each task_progress that carries workflow_progress, and when task_updated or task_notification ends it, a killed one as stopped. The app keeps one stored event per workflow, written again with each, and hangs the run on its call, whose card takes the place of the call's line and ends a folded run the way an ask does. The card reads the name and phases from the script's meta, so it has them before the CLI reports; a workflow still running when the thread is read back in at launch, or when the engine goes, is shown stopped. Checked: workflow.test.ts reads a snapshot's order and states, and WorkflowTests the script's phases and name, a snapshot and the grouping. Frames of the real transcript in the test host of a scratch worktree, with a Workflow call seeded and workflow events routed in: before any, the card had the script's name, "Starting…" and its three phases faint; running, "Verify · 3 of 8 agents done", with Review's three settled beads and two lit ones, Verify's one lit and two faint, and Report faint; done, "Done · 8 agents, 1 failed", the failed verifier's bead red; opened, each agent under its phase with its tool and file, its tools and tokens, or the reason it failed. #### K-151 · An open file is one card in the review
Meriç: an open file shows two circles, the header's and its hunk's, that do the same thing. An open file becomes one card: its header on top and its hunks inside it, parted by space. A file with one hunk has only the header's circle; a file with several keeps a circle per hunk, and the header's marks them all. With it, the review's own findings: the files open, the first look and the keyboard's hunk belong to a thread, not only its folder; a file's section id doesn't change when a new file is staged; a circle that folds a file with a note being written keeps the keys working; the file circle folds the file the keyboard was in; finishing a turn opens only the next file; and the test presses a circle.
Done when: unit tests cover the per-thread first look and the circle paths; frames show an open one-hunk file as one card with one circle and a three-hunk file with its hunks inside; make test passes. Needs Meriç's eye.
Notes: An open file is one card. ReviewScroll keeps its lazy rows: FileHeader, when its file is open, draws the card's top, and each `.unit(unit, alone:, last:)` row draws a square piece of Surface.card under its hunk, the last one rounding the bottom, through `Shape.fileCard(top:bottom:)`, an UnevenRoundedRectangle of radius 12. Hunks sit 4pt inside the card and 4pt apart; the keyboard's hunk, and a folded hunk's line, are lit by the card's tint laid over them again in an 8pt rounded rect, about as bright as Surface.selected. A file with one hunk has only the header's circle, since UnitView drops its own when `alone`. The header pads 16 on each side folded or open, so a click doesn't shift its line. ReviewState has a `thread`: `look(at:for:)` takes over readReview's reset, so another folder starts over while another thread in the same folder keeps the diff until the next read but gets its own open files, keyboard hunk, note and first look. After every read `keepOpenFiles()` lets go of files no longer in the book, and if none are left open the first look comes back, unless you folded them all yourself. A new file's section id reads `A` whether it's untracked or staged, so staging it no longer folds it. `select` folds, through setOpen, the file the keyboard was in and the file of the hunk being left, so a note being written is let go when a circle folds its file, a file's circle folds the keyboard's file, and finishing a turn opens only the next file; unmarking through a circle goes through `select` too. Checked: eachThreadGetsItsOwnFirstLook covers a second thread in one folder, the same thread keeping its files, a read dropping gone files, and files folded by hand staying folded; aCircleMovesOnAndFoldsWhatItLeaves builds a real AppModel and goes through toggleReviewed on hunks and a file, with a note let go, turn 1's end opening only the next file and a file's circle folding the keyboard's file; aNewFileStaysReviewedWhenItsAdded covers the id across `?` and `A`. Frames of the real RootView from K-142's FoldFrames harness in a scratch worktree: TitleCapsule.swift with three hunks as one card with a circle per hunk and the header's, Island.swift's one hunk inside its card with the header's circle alone, ⌥-click showing the cards apart with no seam between a card's pieces. make test passes in a worktree with its Debug app renamed, 77 engine tests and 85 Swift tests. Not checked: the real pointer, clicks and keys, a thread switch in the running app, and a long file scrolling.

#### K-153 · A command's output arrives whole
From the review of K-144. A block drops its process only once the pty has closed, so the last lines of a command that prints and exits at once are kept, the lines Claude reads and the stored event included. What Claude has read is counted in lines of the terminal, not characters, so a screen cleared by a watcher, a trimmed scrollback or a program that took the whole screen doesn't skip or cut what's new. A slash command and Thread › Compact go out on their own, with the unread blocks waiting for the next message. A block run while a reply streams doesn't split the reply. A send that fails leaves its blocks unread.
Done when: ShellTests covers a fast command's tail arriving after its exit, reading after a clear, and a slash command leaving blocks unread; make test passes.
Notes: A block keeps SwiftTerm's LocalProcess past the exit: ShellBlock.letGo waits for the pty to close, which SwiftTerm marks by setting childfd to -1, and for a turn of the main queue that brings no more slices, and only then drops the process and its descriptor. Output after the exit redraws the block, and the new onTail stores it again at most every 80ms, so the screen, the text and the stored `shell` event all get the tail. What Claude has read is a ShellBlock.Mark, counted in terminal rows that keep their numbers as the scrollback trims (ShellRender.numbered), with the line before the last one read remembered: unread() gives the lines after the mark, or the whole current text under the 30,000-character cap when nothing was read, when there are fewer lines than were read, or when that line has been written over, which a clear, a reset or a program taking the whole screen does. The stored event keeps `unread`, how many last lines Claude hasn't read, -1 for all, since a relaunch rebuilds the terminal from the end of the output; an old sentUpTo of -1 reads as -1 and anything else as 0. AppModel.withShells replaces shellContext: it leaves text starting with "/" alone, so `/compact` and Thread › Compact go out as commands and the blocks wait for the next message, and returns a `read` that startTurn calls only once the engine has taken the send. Conversation.shellStarted keeps the open streaming item across the block's insert, so a reply stays one item above the block, as the events' order puts it after a relaunch. Checked: ShellTests adds four. Twenty runs of a real command printing about 306KB and exiting each keep the tail in the text, on the drawn screen and in the stored event, where with `process = nil` put back at the exit the same test fails; reading from a fed terminal holds across a clear of screen and scrollback, a screen-only clear, a scrollback trimmed past 10,000 lines and a program entering and leaving the alternate screen; a slash command goes out alone, and two sends with no engine behind them leave the block unread in memory and in the store; a block run between two text deltas leaves one text item, before and after a relaunch. make test passes in a worktree with its Debug app renamed, 77 engine tests and 87 Swift tests. Not checked: the real window, and a command that has filled SwiftTerm's 4MB backlog when it exits, where SwiftTerm stops reading for good and the rest is lost; the block then keeps its process and looks again about once a second.

ShellCompletion.zsh(_:line:) sorts each group as zsh lists it, -V and nosort aside, drops duplicates, and replaces from where the words start, so `--color=` and `"My N` replace the right text; one match gives zsh's own line and several the list, whose CompletionMenu shows zsh's description faint beside each. #### K-147 · Write while Claude works
In the Claude Code app you can write to Claude while it works, and the message reaches it at its next step without stopping the turn. OriCode wouldn't send while a turn ran: the send circle was Stop and Return did nothing. Now, with something typed, Return or the arrow sends it into the running turn. It shows at once at the end of the transcript as your bubble, its words dimmed and "Waiting for the next step" faint under it, and when Claude takes it up it brightens where it is and Claude's reply follows it. The CLI folds such a message in after the tool that's running returns; if the turn has no step left, the turn ends and the message runs as a turn of its own right after, with the thread working throughout. With nothing typed the circle is Stop, as it is in a thread still waiting on you from before a quit, which has no turn to send into, and ⌘. stops at any time. Stop also cancels whatever still waits, which goes back to the composer oldest first, in front of anything typed, so nothing more runs and nothing written is lost. K-148's queue, built beside this card, takes ⌥Return: Return sends into the turn, ⌥Return holds a message for after it. On Linear as REA-153.
Done when: a message sent with Return or the arrow during a running turn shows in the transcript at once, Claude answers it in the same turn without the turn being stopped, and Stop still stops and puts a message still waiting back in the field.
Notes: The engine pushes a send that arrives during a turn, or while messages sent to one still wait, into the thread's open inbox with the app's id as its uuid, and replies waiting. The CLI reports each message with a uuid in command_lifecycle frames the SDK doesn't type yet; for a waiting id, started becomes message.taken, with newTurn when no turn was running, and any other end becomes message.cancelled: cancelled, the discarded and refused the CLI's schema also lists, or a terminal state with no started before it, which the schema allows. A CLI whose init doesn't list both msg_lifecycle_v1 and interrupt_cancel_queued_v1 refuses a send mid-turn, and the message goes back to the composer with the error, since without those frames it would wait for good; a send that comes before the init waits in the engine until it arrives, and a Stop or the CLI going meanwhile hands it back. A taken message with newTurn starts the engine's turn there, so Stop reaches it before its first word, and a Stop that lands after the last turn's result but before that start still stops it, reported as interrupted. Stop takes each waiting message off the CLI's queue with cancelAsyncMessage, which the SDK has but doesn't type, sending message.cancelled for each it gets back, and then interrupts plainly: the branch passed cancelQueued, which also dropped the reports of background agents, commands and workflows queued beside them. One it can't get back has already gone into the turn the interrupt stops. An interrupt still on its way when a turn ends by itself carries over to the next turn, so a waiting message's turn that it then stops ends as interrupted rather than failing with the CLI's abort. A send with an id that reaches the engine after the turn ended starts a turn like any send and says so with message.taken newTurn, which the app acts on instead of the reply, since the event follows the ended turn's turn.done on the same stream. turn.done says how many still wait, and a CLI that goes away or is closed cancels what waited on it. In the app a waiting message lives in Conversation.waiting, out of the store; taken, it's recorded as a user event after everything so far, keeping its id so its bubble stays put, and a mid-turn one keeps the turn's number and carries midTurn. Item.user's midTurn flag keeps it from starting a turn in the footer's file count, the review's Provenance, the last reply ⌘K copies and K-138's search for the last turn, and K-149's history matches the fourth field. A turn.done with messages waiting keeps Conversation.running on, which is what keeps Stop, the rays and the held-back Finished. Handed-back messages wait in their thread's conversation, each with when it was written, and the composer takes them once none still waits, so the ones a Stop cancels one at a time come back together, oldest first; a released thread that holds any isn't dropped. Images sent alone come back without the question the send put with them. A message into the turn carries the shell's blocks since the last message, as every send does; AppModel.shellReads keeps K-153's read until message.taken, so a message handed back leaves its blocks unread for the next one, though a second message sent while the first still waits carries the same blocks again. A message still waiting when OriCode quits dies with the CLI that held it, and K-138's pickup doesn't carry it. Checked: brought onto main past K-143 and K-149 to K-157 in a worktree, where make test passed with its Debug app renamed, 94 engine tests and 129 Swift tests. midturn.test.ts, sixteen tests against a stand-in CLI, adds a Stop taking a message back by its id with a plain interrupt and doing nothing once nothing waits or runs; a Stop still on its way as the turn ends by itself stopping the next turn rather than failing it, a test that fails with the old reset; a send before the init waiting, then going in or being refused; and a Stop or a close before the init handing it back. MidTurnTests adds a message handed back while another is taken coming back once that one is, and, through a real AppModel with a block run from the prompt, the block read once message.taken is routed and left unread when the send fails. On its branch, before the port and these fixes, the engine was driven on Haiku: a message folded into a two-command turn, Stop with one waiting, and one run as a turn of its own. Not checked: a real CLI since the port, so cancelAsyncMessage, the plain interrupt and the held send have met only the stand-in; typing into the real window mid-turn, the bubble's motion, text coming back into the composer, a message sent just as a turn ends, and a send that fails. Needs Meriç to send a message while a thread runs a command, send one as Claude writes its last words, and press Stop with one waiting.

#### K-148 · Queue the next message
Meriç: while a turn runs, whatever comes next has to wait until the turn is over, and other agent apps let you line it up. K-147 gave Return a job during a turn, sending into it; the queue takes ⌥Return. Now ⌥Return during a turn puts what's typed, and its images, in the thread's queue, and the field clears as it does after a send. The arrow's help says both, "Send now (Return), or after this turn (⌥Return)". With no turn running ⌥Return is a new line, as ⇧Return is. The queue sits inside the composer's glass above the field, one line per message in the order they'll go, its first line in secondary ink with a cross at the end. A click on a line, or ↑ in an empty field for the last one, takes that message back into the field to edit, after what's typed, with its images back among the attachments; with nothing queued ↑ brings back earlier messages as K-149 has it. When a turn ends by itself and nothing sent into it still waits, the first queued message goes out as the thread's next, on screen or not, and the rest wait their turn; Finished comes when the last one ends. A turn that was stopped, failed or cut off by the engine sends nothing more: the queue goes back into the field, and Stop takes K-147's waiting messages back with it, all of them oldest first and ahead of what's typed, so nothing is lost and nothing goes out after Stop. The queue has no Linear issue.
Done when: with a turn running, two messages queued with ⌥Return show in the composer, the first goes out when the turn finishes and the second when that one does, while Return and the arrow still send into the running turn; a queued message can be taken back, by a click or ↑ in an empty field, and removed before it goes; Stop with messages queued and one sent into the turn sends nothing more and puts all their text back in the field, oldest first; make test passes.
Notes: Conversation holds the queue and what it hands back in memory, the way the composer holds its draft, so a quit loses both; nothing about the queue is stored. The hand-back is one channel for this card and K-147: Conversation.handedBack is a list of QueuedMessage, a queued one or a K-147 message the engine cancelled turned into one, kept in the order they were written, and `returning` gives them to the composer only once no message sent into the turn still waits, so a Stop's queue and its cancelled messages come back in one piece. One Composer onChange takes them, and one release guard, holdsMessages, keeps a thread with anything waiting, queued or handed back. QueuedMessage.endedByItself makes the one decision, from turn.done's stopReason and whether an error came in since the turn started: interrupted, engine_stopped, the error subtypes and a turn with an error hand the queue back, and so do the engine stopping, a send the engine turned down, Stop on a thread waiting from before a quit, and a turn Claude's session limit refused, which K-141 reports as `limited` ahead of a result that reads as a finish. That error flag clears on turn.started as well as on a send, so a turn a background agent starts doesn't carry the last one's failure. Stop hands the queue back the moment it's pressed, before the interrupt goes out, because a turn already ending by itself when the interrupt lands would otherwise still send its next. On turn.done, and on a message.cancelled that leaves nothing waiting, AppModel.route has the conversation send its next through send(_:images:in:), the half of send that starts a turn in any thread; sendNext holds while K-147's messages still wait, whether the turn ended with them waiting, which keeps running on, or one is still on its way to the engine. A message send(_:images:in:) refuses, as it does for a thread open in Claude Code, goes back to the field with the rest. The queue waits for turns and for messages sent into them, not for background work: a workflow, background agent or background command still out after the turn doesn't hold it, since the CLI takes their reports in as they come and a server left running would hold the queue for good, so the next message can start while K-143's card and the rays still show that work. Finished waits while the queue's next message runs, and if the engine then turns that send down, a thread you're away from posts the engine's reason instead. Every change to the queue happens under withAnimation(Motion.fade) inside Conversation, so the composer's glass moves with the lines. The lines stop at three and a half and scroll past that, the half line fading while more wait below, and their height comes out of the field's 40%. At the 480pt minimum window one queued line, like a thumbnail, lifts the composer's top under Composer.pickerRoom and the model picker opens below it; that predates the queue and is left to a card of its own. The composer's text isn't keyed by thread, so it takes handed-back text when it shows that thread, now or later. At K-144's shell prompt the button runs the command as before, ↑ leaves the queue alone, a queued line can be removed but not taken back, and nothing handed back comes into the field until the prompt turns back into the composer, so a command being typed is never folded into messages; a command still typed when the prompt closes becomes the message's text, with what was handed back ahead of it. Checked: brought onto K-147 in a worktree of main, where make test passed with its Debug app renamed, 94 engine tests and 145 Swift tests. QueueTests has sixteen: from the branch, the queue's order, a finished turn sending only the first and the second once that one ends, stopped, engine-stopped and two error subtypes handing everything back in order, an error event failing a turn and a turn nobody sent starting clean after one, the engine stopping and a failed send, take back and remove, the field's joined text, a refused send, and a thread not on screen sending its next through AppModel.route with no engine running; new, AppModel.stop on a thread with one message queued, one sent into the turn and one more queued, a turn ending by itself just then sending nothing and the three coming back as one, two, three once the engine's cancel is routed; AppModel.queue refusing with no turn, queueing the trimmed text with its image and emptying the attachments, and AppModel.send going into the turn beside it; the queue holding through a turn.done that left a message waiting until that message's turn ends, and for a message still on its way. Nothing reached a model. Not checked: ⌥Return, ↑, the clicks, hovers and scrolling in the real window, the prompt holding back what's handed back, a real turn sending its queue, the fade, and the lines drawn again after the port; Finished held until the last queued turn ends and handed-back text landing in the field are untested. Needs Meriç to queue two messages with ⌥Return during a turn and watch them go, send one with Return beside them, take one back with ↑, remove one, and press Stop with one queued and one waiting in the turn.

#### K-159 · Heads: what each ray is doing
Meriç: seeing what each agent is doing, the way the Claude Code app lists them, drawn as OriCode draws. While a thread works or waits, its mark sits at the title capsule's left end, the drawer row's mark at the capsule's size, and an idle capsule is unchanged. A click on it, or ⌘I, grows a Heads surface out of the capsule the way ⌘K does, the mark moving into its header on the island's matched geometry, one surface at a time like the others; Esc or a click elsewhere folds it back. A row per head, arriving clockwise with the mark's own stagger: the main loop, its dot alone lit, with its step (thinking, writing, the tool and what on, waiting on you, or waiting on its agents); each agent, a small mark with only its own ray lit, its description, its type when it isn't general-purpose, its current tool and target, and its elapsed time, tokens and tool calls; each workflow with K-143's status and beads, opening to its agents; each background command with a `$` and no ray, its last output line live. Stop on each row under the pointer; a hovered row brightens its ray in the header's mark. A ray lights for an agent or a workflow's running agent only, never for a command, and each head keeps its slot, so row i owns ray i. The engine stops throwing away a subagent's own tool calls and task_progress, and sends a `heads` event, the whole list, in place of `tasks`: membership at once, detail only while the surface is open (`heads.watch`), coalesced to one send in 250ms and none when nothing changed, a command's last line from its output file only while watched; `task.stop` stops one. Heads live outside the transcript's items, so an update redraws one row. On Linear as REA-161, and the tray, K-158, shrinks to your own blocks, a second group in the same surface, if it's still wanted.
Done when: engine tests cover a subagent's tool call reaching its head and a `heads` event coalesced; frames show the capsule's mark growing into the surface with a main loop, two agents at their tools, a workflow and a command, and a head ending; a head stops from its row; idle cost with the surface closed measures as it did before (rule 6); make test passes. Needs Meriç's eye.

K-160 to K-163 come from a measured audit on 2026-09-26, for rule 6. Idle, the app and the engine already sit at 0% CPU and under one wakeup a second; the costs are after a turn, at launch, while streaming and in the bundle.
Notes: engine/heads.ts holds a thread's heads: every non-ambient task the CLI starts, with its kind from task_type (local_agent, remote_agent and in_process_teammate are agents, local_bash and monitor tasks commands, local_workflow a workflow, anything else other), its call, label, subagent type, background, depth and start. thread.ts now hands it a subagent's own assistant frames by parent_tool_use_id, where it used to drop them, and every tool_result, and trackTask passes it the task messages; `heads` replaces the `tasks` event. Membership goes out at once; tokens and tool calls from task_progress, the step, and a command's last line go out only while `heads.watch` is on, a change setting one 250ms setTimeout with no interval, a list equal to the last one sent skipped, and a background Bash result's output file followed with fs.watch only while watched, its last 4KB read and colour codes stripped through shell.ts's lastLine. The step is `stepOf`, the tool plus a path relative to the thread's folder or the command's first line, pattern, url or query, in K-143's lastTool and lastDetail shape. `task.stop` calls the SDK's stopTask. In the app, Heads.swift keeps each Head as an @Observable object in a Heads store on Conversation, outside the items and never stored, so an update redraws one row. Heads gives out the six rays: an agent keeps the lowest free ray, a workflow holds one for each running agent in its `workflow` events, a head arriving with all six taken waits for one, commands and other tasks never hold one, and a head that ends lets its ray go out at once and keeps it until its row has faded 450ms later. Counts reach the screen at most once a second. RaysMark takes `slots` and a `focus`, the dot or a head's rays, and its slow turn asks for 10 to 30 frames a second where it had no range. The dot is now the main loop: ThreadMark lights it while a turn runs and dims it to 0.28 when only agents work, and no ray stands for the main loop any more. The drawer row and the capsule's left end draw ThreadMark at 14pt while the thread works; a click on it, ⌘I, or Show heads in ⌘K grows HeadsSurface out of the capsule as a fourth island piece, the mark on the island's matched geometry into its header, which has the thread's title and a tally. A Main loop row says Conversation.mainStep, read off the last item: Thinking, Writing, the open call and its target through the new ToolSummary.target, Waiting on you, or Waiting on N agents. Each head's row: an agent's mark with only its rays lit, its type unless general-purpose, its step, and Text(.timer), tokens and tool calls with numericText; a workflow's WorkflowCard.status and Beads, opening to AgentLine; a command's `$` and last line. Rows come in 40ms apart after the glass starts, steps cross-fade, an ending row dims and the list closes up, Stop takes the counts' place under the pointer, and the hovered row focuses the header's mark. WorkflowCard's mark lights the rays its agents hold, and Beads, Bead and AgentLine are internal. Checked: heads.test.ts, nine tests against a stand-in CLI: a subagent's Read reaching its head's step while watched and never the transcript; three task_progress frames sent once after 250ms and a repeat not sent; nothing unwatched, detail at once on watch and dropped on unwatch; a command's line followed from its output file only while watched; kinds, ambient tasks left out and ends; stop and an already-gone stop; a CLI going clearing its heads; stepOf and outputFile. HeadsTests has eight: routing with detail kept while unwatched, rays for agents only, a kept ray and a freed one reused after the fade, a seventh agent waiting for a ray, a workflow holding a ray per running agent, the engine going ending every head, and the main loop's step. Frames of the real RootView with seeded heads, two agents at their tools, a workflow, a dev server and the main loop on a Grep: the capsule's mark growing into the surface, an agent ending, its row dimmed then gone and the tally 2 to 1, the fold back, and the drawer row's mark, whose enlargements show a ray per agent and two for the workflow's running agents. make test passes in a worktree with its Debug app renamed, 103 engine tests and 153 Swift tests. Rule 6: with the surface closed, a finished thread open and the window behind others, top over 30 seconds twice on each build gave the app 0.00% CPU, no idle wakeups and 0.13 to 0.30 context switches a second before and after, and the engine 0.00% CPU and about 0.4 context switches a second both times. Not checked: a real CLI, so the subagent frames, task_progress and stopTask have met only the stand-in, and that the SDK forwards a subagent's assistant frames without forwardSubagentText rests on the old code's early returns; Stop from a row and the hover focus in the real window; the mark's travel into the header, which captures 200ms apart show only mid-growth; ⌘I from the menu; Reduce Motion; the 20fps turn and the ray-per-agent marks at 14pt. Needs Meriç's eye on the surface and on a main loop that no longer lights a ray.

#### K-160 · Quiet after the turn and at launch
The engine keeps a thread's CLI for 5 to 6 minutes after its turn, and an idle CLI measured 66 context switches and 4 wakeups a second, 0.5% CPU and 136MB: it lets go after 90 seconds, and at once for a thread that isn't open while the window is hidden. Every launch spawns `claude auth status` and four CLIs, the models probe, two defaults probes and the usage probe, 974MB at their peak: the models list and defaults are kept keyed by `claude --version` and refreshed in the background at most daily, and usage waits for the hover that already asks for it. A launch with a folder starts two engines: boot starts one, and Engine.start can't be entered twice across its await. The review is read on every activation, thread switch and turn end with six git processes, and colours every hunk even while it's closed, which loads JavaScriptCore at launch in any folder with changes: it colours only while shown, skips a read when HEAD, the index and the files' times haven't moved, asks git once where it asked six times, and shares one highlighter. A defaults write that isn't a setting, the window's frame, doesn't bump the settings' revision, and the idle CLIs and probes run below user-initiated QoS.
Done when: before and after, measured and in the Notes: CLIs spawned at launch and their peak memory (5, 974MB), open to engine ready (750 to 850ms), engine and children's wakeups in the ten minutes after a turn, the app's memory at launch in a changed folder (51MB with 15MB of JavaScriptCore), and engines after opening a folder on the Dock icon (2); make test passes.
Notes: The engine lets a thread's CLI go 90 seconds after its turn instead of 5 to 6 minutes, and at once for a thread that isn't open while the window can't be seen: `releaseIdle` in engine/idle.ts sets the rule, `Thread.onIdle` and `idleLeft` feed it, and a CLI let go ends its heads and workflows through `Thread.endHeads`, the way one that goes by itself does, with `Heads.clear` cancelling its pending send, so no watcher or timer outlives it. main.ts's `letGo` keeps no fixed timer, only one for the next CLI due to go, so nothing wakes while no CLI is up. The app tells the engine which thread is open and whether the main window's occlusion state is visible, through a new `window` method, from `AppModel.tellWindow`. Hello keeps the SDK's model list in `Application Support/<build>/Engine/models.json` (engine/cache.ts, `Cache`, passed in as ORICODE_CACHE), keyed by `claude --version`, and the `models` event's defaults keyed by a hash of the hello list and the user's settings.json, so a catalog or settings change probes again; a defaults reading is kept only when the settings answered, since a probe can miss a model to a 429. A launch that finds the list a day old reads it again in the background after a minute; a new or updated Claude Code probes before hello answers, as before. The launch's usage probe is gone: the hover and a turn's end still ask, so the glass is empty until one does. `AppModel.boot` runs once and `Engine.start` joins a start already under way; `Engine` takes a `findNode` for tests. `git.diff` starts with one `git status --porcelain=v2 --branch` plus a stat of each path it lists, and answers `same` for the mark the app sends back; a full read is status, one `diff --raw --numstat -z` and the patch, 3 git processes where there were 6, and the repository's top is cached per folder. `ReviewState.builtFor` rebuilds on `same` only for another thread. Hunks are coloured only while the review is shown, and when it opens. `CodeHighlighter` holds the one `Highlightr` behind a lock, which `TranscriptCodeHighlighter` uses too. `defaultsRevision` moves only when one of the eight keys the starting choices read changes, not on the window's frame. The engine runs at `.utility` instead of `.userInitiated`. Checked, measured on a renamed build before and after: CLIs at launch went from 5 (`auth status`, the models probe, two defaults probes, usage), peaking at 912 to 983MB over three launches, to 1, `auth status`, peaking at 73 to 123MB over five, with 4 at 739MB on the first launch after a Claude Code update or with no cache; open to hello from 1056, 1360 and 1461ms (1166 opened with a folder) to 488, 633 and 1097ms (581 and 667); the ten minutes after a turn, the engine's own Thread code with a real CLI and nothing sent, from 21.0 context switches and 1.32 idle wakeups a second with the CLI let go at 5:00 to 9.0 and 0.16 with it let go at 1:30, an idle CLI and its child costing 40 to 60 a second and the engine alone 0.45; the app at launch in a changed folder from 57MB, 18MB of it JavaScriptCore, to 37 to 38MB with none; engines after `open -a` with a folder from 2 to 1; the engine's priority from 31 to 20, with CPU-bound work 266 to 298ms at utility against 269 to 347ms before. Resuming a real session with nothing sent took 350ms for a 21.7MB session and 277ms for a 240KB one, against 233 to 260ms for a fresh start; doing so appended mode and cost-state lines to those two session files of Meriç's and nothing else. A new idle test lets a CLI go with the heads watched, a head tailing a real file and a send pending, and finds heads, timer and watcher gone. make test passes in a worktree with its Debug app renamed, 114 engine tests and 155 Swift tests. Not checked: the `window` message from a real hidden or minimised window, covered by the idle tests and a protocol test; the review's colours arriving as it opens and its counts on screen, covered by ReviewTests; the daily re-read a day later; git processes counted live on activation; and the after-turn numbers with the QoS change, since both runs were at default QoS.

#### K-162 · Terminal blocks that let go
A running block's redraw walks the whole scrollback every 80ms, 15ms a tick at 10,000 lines, a fifth of a core: it renders only the last 400 rows it shows and counts lines as they come. Ended blocks are never let go, each keeping its terminal, up to 65MB for a full one: an ended, stored block keeps its screen and output and drops its terminal and view. A block whose pty a background job holds open wakes once a second for as long as it's held: it waits on the pty closing instead. K-156's warm zsh ends after ten idle minutes and comes back with the next prompt.
Done when: before and after, measured and in the Notes: draw ms per tick at 10,000 lines, the app's memory after twenty commands of 10,000 lines, and the app's wakeups while a background job holds a block's pty; make test passes.
Notes: ShellRender.tail reads a running block's last 400 lines up from the end of the buffer and gives back the row the first starts on, and ShellRender.LineCount counts the lines above that as rows scroll off, reading each row's wrap flag once and dropping it when the scrollback trims it; a clear of the scrollback, a reset, a resize or the other buffer starts the count again, noticed by the buffer, the columns, the top going backwards, or the last row counted being another BufferLine or a new generation of it. ShellRender.attributed builds the plain text whole and dresses only the stretches that differ from the block's ink, offset by unicode scalars; built a stretch at a time, those 400 lines had cost a redraw about 5ms. ShellBlock's view and terminal are optional now. letGo waits on a DispatchSource read source on the pty instead of polling childfd once a second: output wakes it, and while there's output SwiftTerm hasn't read (FIONREAD) it suspends until SwiftTerm's next slice; nothing to read means the pty has closed, and it waits for SwiftTerm to mark the end, then a turn of the main queue with no new slices, and draws once more. Then onClosed has AppModel.settle store the block and call ShellBlock.dropTerminal, which lets go of the terminal and BlockTerminalView and keeps the screen, the output and the last lines that fit in ShellContext.limit with their row numbers, so unread(), unreadLines, read(to:) and text work unchanged. When the block's thread is gone, settle drops its shellBlocks and shellsInView entries, and endShells(of:) drops the ended ones at once. BlockPanel draws the pane only while the block has its view; only a running block opens. ShellBlockView's replay of a stored block uses tail and LineCount too. ZshCompletion keeps one DispatchWorkItem, put off by every Tab, and after ten minutes without one hangs zsh up and removes its temp ZDOTDIR, which also goes when zsh exits and at quit; the prompt opening starts another. A background job doesn't hold a block's pty on this Mac: when the shell exits macOS takes the terminal back from any job it left, `sleep 600 &`, `&!`, `& disown`, `nohup` and a HUP-ignoring subshell alike, the job's later writes failing, so the old loop polled forever only where SwiftTerm had stopped reading under its 4MB backlog, which now waits without waking. Checked: draw per tick at 10,000 lines, in the test host built -O with the old and new paths side by side, from 19.4 to 19.6ms to 3.0 to 3.2ms, and at 200 lines from 2.6 to 2.9ms to 1.35 to 1.45ms, the count costing 0.005ms a tick where counting from scratch cost 1.1ms. The test host's memory after twenty `seq` commands of 10,000 lines went from +660MB to +81MB, every block having let go of its terminal. Context switches in a quiet harness running ShellBlock alone for 20s with `sleep 600 &!` left behind were 0.35 a second before and after, as with no job. The old and new attributed gave equal AttributedStrings over colours, bold, underline, dim, wide and combining characters and a flag. ShellTests adds three: tail and LineCount matching a whole read through wrapped lines, a trimmed scrollback, two clears, a program taking the whole screen and a resize to 60 columns; an ended block letting go of its terminal, keeping its 3,000-line count and screen, giving Claude its lines once and going with its deleted thread; and `sleep 30 & disown` not holding the block. K-153's twenty-run test now also checks every block let go with its tail in its text and screen, and CompletionTests adds zshGoesWhenNobodyTabs. make test passes in a worktree with its Debug app renamed, 94 engine tests and 149 Swift tests. Not checked: the real window, a real SwiftTerm 4MB backlog at exit, ten real idle minutes, gitstatusd going with the hang-up, a zsh config that ignores it, and temp ZDOTDIRs earlier launches left in $TMPDIR.

#### K-163 · A smaller app
The Release app is 13.4MB. Highlightr's 250 themes ship for the one it uses (1.1MB), and highlight.js with every language where the app names about 25 (about 0.8MB); Sparkle's XPC services are for sandboxed apps, which OriCode isn't (0.4MB), and its nibs and 36 localisations go unused under OriCode's own update driver (0.36MB); the icon's PNGs are recompressed. Each goes in a build step, not by hand in a package.
Done when: `du` of the Release app before and after (13.4MB, aiming near 10.5), with highlighting and Check for Updates… still working; make test passes.
Notes: scripts/prune.sh takes out of a Release build what Sparkle and Highlightr ship and OriCode never uses, and `make app` and `make release` run it after xcodebuild and before codesign. It can't be a build phase: Xcode embeds Sparkle and copies Highlightr's bundle outside the phases, and on an incremental build it copied and signed Sparkle again after a Prune phase had run. From Sparkle it takes the XPCServices, the two nibs and all 36 lproj folders: Sparkle reaches for the XPC services only when the Info.plist sets SUEnableInstallerLauncherService or SUEnableDownloaderService, which OriCode's doesn't, so it downloads in process and installs through SUInstallerLauncher, Autoupdate and Updater.app, which stay; the nibs are loaded only by SUUpdateAlert and SUUpdatePermissionPrompt, and every Base.lproj string equals its key, so Sparkle's errors read the same. From Highlightr it keeps atom-one-dark and pojoaque, which Highlightr() loads first and returns nil without, and it cuts highlight.min.js, v11.11.1 as Highlightr ships it, from 192 languages to 27 by dropping each language's self-registering part: the 24 CodeHighlighter names, plus diff, shell and plaintext for Markdown fences; a kept language that isn't found fails the build. The icon is unchanged: actool stores each size as lzfse ARGB, so recompressing the PNGs gave a byte-identical Assets.car, and the 1024px icon costs 591KB however it's written. Checked: `du -sk` of a Release build from the Makefile's app recipe in a folder of its own, signed ad hoc, went from 13,836KB to 11,024KB: Sparkle from 2,600 to 1,904 (XPCServices 324 to 0, Resources 364 to 8), Highlightr's bundle from 2,156 to 132, the binary 6,020, the engine 1,764 and Assets.car 1,068 unchanged; an incremental build pruned to the same size, and `codesign --verify --deep --strict` passes on both. Highlightr's own code, built against the pruned files and the package's, gave the same Atom One Dark colours for Swift, TypeScript, JSON, bash, shell, diff, an unknown lua and an unnamed block; Highlightr() fell from 26ms to 11ms, and auto-detecting 200 unlabelled lines from 555ms to 97ms. make test passes in a worktree with its Debug app renamed, 103 engine tests and 153 Swift tests. Not checked: a real Sparkle update, which only a release shows; the pruned Release app itself, since its bundle id is the installed OriCode's; highlighting in the real window; and a fence naming a language outside the 27, which now falls back to auto-detection over those 27.

#### K-161 · Streaming and typing under a frame
A streaming reply re-renders its whole Markdown and re-highlights a growing code block on every delta, about 12ms a delta by the end of a 12,000-character reply, past a 120Hz frame; code shows plain while its item is live and is highlighted once it settles, and deltas reach the view at most once a frame. RootView re-runs on every delta because it reads `items.isEmpty`, and it and the composer fetch the chat and project from SwiftData on every body, seven times a keystroke in the composer: a stored flag and the selected chat and project kept until the selection or the store changes. route fetches the chat per event and sets the Dock badge per delta: a map from thread to chat, and the badge only when its count changes. The transcript asks for the waiting ask once per entry and decodes images on every render. ⌘K fetches and decodes every message on each keystroke and arrow key, 15ms at 339 messages and growing: an index built once and kept up by record. Opening a 1,029-event thread takes 60ms on the main thread. Whether autosave rewrites the growing text event on every delta is measured and, if it does, stopped.
Done when: before and after, measured and in the Notes: main-thread ms per delta on a replayed 12,000-character reply with code (about 12 now), SwiftData fetches and ms per composer keystroke, ⌘K ms per keystroke at 339 and about 10,000 messages, and thread-open ms for the 1,029-event thread; make test passes.
Notes: A streaming reply reaches the view at most once a frame: `Conversation.hold` adds a delta straight to the open item when the last was shown a frame or more ago, at the screen's `minimumRefreshInterval`, and otherwise holds it for the next frame; `record`, `flush` and everything else show held text first, so a tool call or a block still lands after the whole text, and the stored event's payload is rewritten once per frame shown, not per delta. While a reply streams its code shows plain through `StreamingCodeHighlighter`, and each block that has closed is coloured in the background as it closes, so the reply has its colours when it settles. `TranscriptCodeHighlighter` colours off the main thread: a block with no colours yet shows plain, and the code block that asked reads that block's observable entry, so it's drawn again when they're in; the cache holds 300 blocks and drops the one used longest ago instead of emptying itself. `Conversation` keeps a stored `started` and `waitingAsk`, looked up again only when an ask comes or goes, and RootView, the Island, the composer and the transcript read those instead of `items`, so a delta no longer redraws them. AppModel keeps `projects` until `revision` moves and `chat` until the revision or the selection does, so typing fetches nothing. `route` finds the thread through `conversations` with `chat(withID:)`, a deleted thread's conversation goes with it, and `Notifier.badge` sets the Dock only when its count changes. The transcript works out the waiting ask once per body and caches decoded images by message id in an `NSCache`. ⌘K searches `MessageIndex`, which reads every `user` and `text` event once, off the main thread, at the first search, and from then `Conversation.record` adds new messages and `showHeld` keeps a streaming reply current; messages are NSStrings, about 20 times faster to search than Swift Strings, with the dotless ı folded ahead of time, `MessageSearch.holds` matching and `MessageSearch.snippet` still cutting; a query that only adds to the last searches only its hits, past 2,000 messages the search uses every core, and `paletteMessages` works its rows out once per query, so an arrow key asks nothing new. Opening a thread reads and decodes its events as `StoredEvent`s on a context of its own, off the main thread, and replays them on main, RootView laying the thread out as started meanwhile from `Chat.started` so the composer doesn't move; an engine event or a send that needs the conversation first makes it at once and the read is dropped. Checked, as main-thread CPU on the Debug test host with the real RootView in an offscreen window and synthetic events through `model.route`: a 12,000-character reply with code in 300 deltas of 40 characters, one every 10ms, from 14.7ms a delta (19.8 over the last 30, 4.4s in all) to 4.6 (4.8, 1.4s), and one every 2ms from 10.3 (15.2, 3.1s) to 2.2 (1.8, 0.67s); the frame the reply settles in from 15 to 16ms to 62 to 68ms, since that's where its code takes its colours; a composer keystroke from 13.0 to 13.9ms with two SwiftData fetches to 11.5 to 12.3ms with none; ⌘K at 339 messages from 54ms a key to 11.8 to 13.4, arrows from 52 to 6 to 9, and at 10,000 from 1,452ms to 14.3, the worst key 29.7, arrows from 1,459 to 16; a 1,029-event thread from 76 to 113ms of main thread to select, 286ms to its first render, to 0.6ms, its events arriving 86 to 95ms later from the background, a 1.8ms replay, and 215 to 231ms to the first render, nearly all of it drawing the last 200 items. Autosave doesn't write on every delta: with it on, no save came in 6.8s of streaming or the 2s after, before and after. Frames show a live reply's code plain and both blocks coloured 30ms after it settles. StreamingTests has ten, among them deltas in one frame arriving together, a tool call after held text stored and replayed, narrowing matching a fresh search at every key with ı folded, and a thread read off the main thread or needed during its read. make test passes in a worktree with its Debug app renamed, 114 engine tests and 165 Swift tests. Not checked: Release timings, a real turn streaming, the settle frame on a real screen, a real thread opening, images through the cache and the Dock badge. The settle frame and a keystroke still past 8.3ms go on to K-164.

#### K-164 · A reply settles, and a key lands, inside a frame
Two costs K-161 measured and left. The frame a reply settles in, where its code takes its colours, went from 15 to 62 to 68ms in the Debug host, since coloured code is laid out all at once there: a reply's finished parts, each paragraph and code block, are drawn as views of their own, so a delta or a settle lays out only what changed, and a block's colours arrive on it alone. A composer keystroke still takes 11.5 to 12.3ms with no fetch left in it: what runs per key is found and cut until a key costs under a 120Hz frame, 8.3ms. Both are measured in a Release build under a name of its own, as well as Debug, since Release is what people run.
Done when: before and after, measured in Release and in the Notes: the settle frame of a 12,000-character reply with code, ms per composer keystroke, and ms per delta while streaming, none past 8.3ms; make test passes.
Notes: A reply is now `Reply` (App/Reply.swift): its text is cut where cmark-gfm, which MarkdownUI is built on, starts each top-level block, with the table extension attached, and each block is an Equatable `ReplyBlock` holding its own `Markdown`, keyed by its index, so a block that hasn't changed isn't parsed or laid out again. Only the block still growing goes through `StreamingCodeHighlighter`, so a settle redraws only the last block and each code block takes its colours on its own. MarkdownUI's block margins are internal, so the theme's `blockMargin` sets MarkdownUI's margin and a `ReplyMargin` preference at once, and Reply spaces blocks by MarkdownUI's rule, the larger of a block's top and the previous block's bottom. cmark-gfm gives a table's header paragraph no line numbers, so `Reply.split` works the table's start back from its rows; text that defines a link reference stays whole. The split is cached per reply in an `NSCache` keyed by the item's id and text. `StreamingCodeHighlighter.isOpen` reads the block's own text, closed once its last line is a fence with the code right before it, and `TranscriptCodeHighlighter.prepare` is gone. In the composer, what's typed lives in an `@Observable` `Draft`, bound with `Bindable(draft).text`, which the composer's body never reads: the field carries the onChange of the text and the slash query, the slash menu and send button sit in an `Isolated` view, and the button reads `Draft.blank`, which changes only between empty and not, so a key no longer runs `Composer.body` (185 samples to 1 over 600 keys under Time Profiler). project.yml declares swift-cmark's `cmark-gfm` and `cmark-gfm-extensions`, which MarkdownUI already resolves. Checked, as main-thread CPU with the real RootView offscreen and synthetic events through `model.route`, before and after in one session: the frame a 12,000-character reply with code settles in went from 50.8 and 62.3ms to 21.2 and 31.4 in the Debug app, from 48.3 and 64.0 to 23.5 and 34.2 in a Release build under its own name, and from 63.0 to 19.8 to 21.4 in the test host, what's left being the bench's last block, a 3,600-character code block still open at the end, taking its colours; CPU in the 300ms after the settle from 64 to 70ms to 34 to 42ms. A composer key went from 9.7, 8.4 and 9.4ms to 8.2, 7.2 and 8.4 in Debug and from 10.5, 8.5 and 9.6 to 9.0, 7.4 and 8.8 in Release over three rounds of 30 keys, an earlier paired session reading 6.6 to 7.2 in Release after, and the test host 9.3 to 9.9. Streaming got slower: at a delta every 10ms, from 4.34 to 5.30ms in Debug and 3.89 to 4.82 in Release, and at 2ms from 1.86 to 2.24 and 1.68 to 1.85. The split itself costs about 0.03ms a delta; the rest comes with the new StreamingCodeHighlighter, where K-161's version measured 4.46 with the split but sent every stale partial code block to Highlightr in the background, about 186 runs a reply; a uniform 13% slowdown with no new hot spot suggests the clock, not verified. On Meriç's 60Hz Air, 16.7ms a frame, a key and a delta fit one frame before and after, and the settle drops from three or four frames to two. ReplyTests has three: blocks cut at each start through a loose list, a fence with blank lines, a table after a paragraph, a setext heading and an open fence; a link definition keeping the reply whole; and a varied reply through Reply coming to one Markdown's height within half a point, which fails when the spacing rule is broken. A varied reply's frame and a settled frame are pixel-identical before and after. make test passes in a worktree with its Debug app renamed, 114 engine tests and 172 Swift tests. Not met: the settle frame and the deltas under 8.3ms, and a key under 8.3ms in the test host; K-165 takes them. Not checked: a real screen or turn, ProMotion, selection and links by hand, the app's size with the cmark products declared, and a real reply defining a link.

#### K-166 · Limits you can see coming
Meriç: the limit should say it's a usage limit, look like the app, and let you choose whether the thread goes on by itself. K-141's line becomes a card for a thread's latest limit, on the card surface with no stroke: the usage glass larger and full to its rim in the Band's red, the limit's name (Session limit, Weekly limit), "Resets at 23:30" and a countdown the system draws once a minute without re-running the view, and a native Toggle, "Go on when it resets", in Cancel's place, which can arm the wait again as well as call it off. Older limits keep their line. Settings gets "Go on when a limit resets", on by default for the session limit, and the card's toggle overrides it per thread, a weekly limit included; the notification says "It goes on at …" only when armed. "Rate limited" goes: a refusal the CLI names without a reset says it was a usage limit, and a plain 429 says Claude is limiting requests for a moment. Before the limit, the engine forwards the CLI's own `rate_limit_event`, which it now drops, as `limits` when its status or percent changes, and `system/informational` notices as notes: the usage glass reads that push during a turn, where it was stale until the turn ended, and replaces the probe the engine spawned after every turn; an `allowed_warning` puts one faint line in the thread per window, "90% of the session used, resets in 1 hr 10 min", amber or red only on the number. Claude Code 2.1.282 has a wrap-up near the limit ("Approaching your 5-hour usage limit - Claude will wrap up the current step."), behind Anthropic's own flag and off for this account, with no setting to turn on; OriCode adds no toggle for it and shows its notice when it comes. Nothing polls or animates while waiting (rule 6).
Done when: engine tests cover `limits` sent on a change and not on a repeat, and a notice becoming a note; Swift tests cover the setting and the card's toggle arming and calling off the wait; frames show the card for a session and a weekly limit, armed and not, and the near-limit line; a turn's end spawns no usage probe; make test passes. Needs Meriç's eye.
Notes: The engine forwards the CLI's rate_limit_event as `limits`: `limitsOf` in engine/thread.ts gives fractions used and resets in milliseconds, with a `windows` list read from the CLI's undocumented unifiedWindows, and `limitsKey` holds a report back unless a status, a reset or a whole percent has moved. Claude Code 2.1.283 sends unifiedWindows with each window's fraction and on `allowed` drops rateLimitType and utilization, so during most of a turn only `windows` carries the session's reading. `system/informational` at notice and warning comes to the thread as a `note`, which Conversation records and shows as a note line; info and suggestion are dropped. "Rate limited" is gone: a refusal the CLI calls rejected with no reset says "Stopped at one of Claude's usage limits.", and a plain 429 says "Claude is limiting requests for a moment. Try again shortly." `AppModel.takeLimits` puts the pushed readings into `PlanUsage` through `PlanUsage.take`, keeping only the windows the probe names under the same labels, so the glass follows the turn, and turn.done no longer spawns a probe; the hover still asks for the whole picture, and `refreshUsage` and the engine's `usage` lost `fresh`. Near a limit, Conversation stores one `limits` event per window until the reset it named has passed, which replays as `NearLimitLine`, "90% of the session used · resets in 1 hr 10 min", in secondary ink with only the number in Band's colour, the span taken from when it was said. `LimitCard` is the thread's latest limit on Surface.card at radius 14: `GlassLevel` at 34pt full in Band's red, the limit's name, "Resets at 23:30", the weekday for a weekly one, and a countdown drawn by the system with `Text(.currentDate, format: .offset(to:allowedFields:sign:))`, since `.reference` rounds 69 minutes to an hour and `Text(timerInterval:)` ticks every second; its native Toggle, "Go on when it resets", calls `AppModel.goOn`, which arms `Conversation.resume(at:)` for any limit, weekly included, or calls the wait off, and a `TimelineView(.explicit([resetsAt]))` draws it once more at the reset as "Reset at …" with the glass dimmed and no toggle. `GlassLevel` is the glass UsageGlass drew, now shared. Older limits keep `LimitLine`'s text without a control. Settings › Conversation has a new heading, At a usage limit, with "Go on when a limit resets" (`goOnWhenLimitResets`, on); off, `limited` doesn't arm resumeAt. The line a thread is sent names its limit (`AppModel.limitLine(_:)`), and ↑ history leaves out anything ending in `limitLineEnd`. The away notification comes from `Conversation.turnLimit`: "It goes on at …" when armed, otherwise "Resets at …". While waiting nothing runs but K-141's one sleep and that one TimelineView date. Checked: limit.test.ts went from 4 to 9 tests, covering `limits` as fractions and milliseconds with its windows, sent again only on a status or whole-percent move, a report with no windows, notice and warning becoming notes and info and suggestion not, and the two new lines; LimitTests from 5 to 11, covering the setting off leaving a session limit unarmed, the card's toggle arming a weekly limit and calling it off, the latest turn's limit until the next turn, a near-limit line said once per window and not again after a replay, a reset window said again, and the glass taking a pushed 0.42 then 0.72, Band.critical. Frames of the real RootView offscreen show the near-limit lines, a session card armed and not, a weekly card beside an older session line, and a passed card. make test passes in a worktree with its Debug app renamed, 119 engine tests and 178 Swift tests. Not checked: a real limit or allowed_warning, unifiedWindows from a live CLI rather than read out of the 2.1.283 binary, the wrap-up notice, whose flag is off for this account, the toggle and the Settings pane in the real window, the notification's words on screen, and a streaming turn measured before and after, though a turn now spawns one probe fewer.

Meriç, before release: `sleep 20` run from the prompt, opened full with Open, then stopped, leaves the panel up; Close and ⌘J don't put it away, and Esc takes several presses. A block that ends while open, stopped from its header, with ⌃C typed into it or by finishing on its own, puts itself back in the thread at once, and Close, ⌘J, a click on the transcript and one Esc each put an open block away every time, before and after it ends.

Notes: A block that ended while open, stopped from its header, with ⌃C or by finishing, left its panel and terminal drawn over the thread with `openBlocks` empty and `openShell` nil, so Close, ⌘J and Esc found nothing to close, and only something else RootView reads, the drawer say, took the panel away; vim quitting did the same. The close the pty's callbacks brought about ran in the same turn of the main queue as the block's own changes and the transcript scrolling to them, and RootView never drew again after it: with that scroll turned off, or the close a turn later, the panel went, while closing without animation still left it. `AppModel+Shell` now puts a block back through `putBack`, which closes it a turn of the main queue later, and a program taking the screen again in that turn keeps its block. A click on the transcript didn't put even a running block away, since around the panel the transcript is blank scroll view whose clicks never reach the conversation's tap: RootView lays a clear button, hidden from accessibility, behind the panel down to its bottom edge, which closes the block. Before, all twelve runs of Stop, ⌃C or finishing followed by Close, ⌘J, a transcript click or Esc ended with openShell nil and the panel and terminal still drawn, which neither the action nor five more Escs removed, and vim's `:q!` left the same; after, the panel and terminal are gone the moment the block ends in every run, with the keyboard in the composer. A running block: Close and ⌘J put it away before and after, a transcript click did nothing before and puts it away after, and Esc stays the program's, per K-146. ShellTests adds aBlockEndingWhileOpenLetsGoOfTheWindow, which hosts RootView, stops an open `sleep 5` and expects openShell nil, the composer given the keyboard and no terminal in the window; it fails before the fix and passes after. make test passes in a worktree with its Debug app renamed, 114 engine tests and 173 Swift tests.
